THE TIME TO HATCH.

Birds intended for exhibition and for spring• chickens should. be hatched at different times from those for laying and for stock purposes. For the latter objects the- Wile for hatching non-sitters and the heavy breeds also differs. The light breeds, such as the Minorca, Leg- horn, Ancona, Hataburgh, and others of kin- dred type need not be hatched until s;x or seven' weeks after such varieties as the Wyan- dotte, Plymouth Rook, Orpington, Langshan, and Rhode Island Red. The latter breeds do not mature so quickly as those of the former type; they must therefore ;be hatched consider- ably earlier. Attila same time, they-Must not be hatched boo early, for :if this is so, iii all probability they will moult -in the late summer and consequently will not- commence to lay until the time when the highest prices are over and are oa the downward grade. On the other hand, if they are hatched too late in the sea- son they have not reached the laying stage in their maturity before the cold weather com- mences, and their ".coming on" is very much retarded. There is a right time to aim for, namely, when the first year's moult' may he 4,voided, and yet maturity will be advanced to the point of laying-- For the .consummation of- this ideal the heavy breeds should be hatched in relays. The first lot should appear the last week in February, continuing on throughout March until the second •-week of. April.

===== 0002601_19150306_art0010.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SCRATCHING SHED.

A scratching shed attached to the house is a tremendous boon, for it enables the hens to obtain the necessary exercise without ventur- ing into the open. Fowls have a great objec- tion to sheltering in their roosting compart- ments during the daytime, and on many occa- sions I have seen a flock of birds standing about in the open on wet and cold days rather than take shelter in the sleeping quarters. A scratching shed, which can be erected -very cheaply indeed, does not tequire to be built at all elaborately, all that is needed being a back, roof, and two. ends. The front, may be quite open, or else may be covered; with wire netting; the latter plan, I think, is the better. The fiCtoi' should be covered with straw or chaff litter, and instead. of feeding grain from a trough it should be thrown amongst the litter, for this affords the birds several hours of exer- cise in scratching for their food. . On wet or very cold days, when the fowls are all 'the better under cover, only half rations of soft food should be fed first thing in the morning, followed Shortly after With some, grain thrown down amongst the litter. This keeps the birds busily occupied till about mid-day, and pre- vents them getting into mischief and contract- ing such habits as feather-picking otr egg-eat- ing., Again, about 2 p.m. or 2.30, Pan. some more grain should be scattered, giving employ- ment to the birds till they go t? roost.—" Farm and Home."

===== 0002601_19150306_art0011.txt | included=true | included =====
GRADING.

The greatest. obstacle in the way of improve- ment in the marketing of eggs in this country is the present practice of selling on a basis of quantity rather than quality. It is quite a common occurrence for eggs delivered as new- laid country eggs to include a large proportion of second-grade and even stale eggs. With a system of grading this would not be possible. On the Continent and in Ireland eggs are graded into three sizes (firsts, seconds and thirds), and the inferior and small eggs are retained for sale locally. Such a system, effi- ciently carried_ out, has numerous advantages, of which the following may he mentioned. Poultry-keepers whose aim is to produce only eggs of the best quality wofild Obtain an in- creased price, and retailers could make their purchases with the knowledge that the eggs are what they are stated to be. Uniformity in sae means that supplies of graded eggs will present an attractive appearance, and also that there is less risk of breakages in packing. With the prospect of obtaining increased prices thete would he a tendency in the direction of a general improvement in the quality of the eggs produced.. .

===== 0002601_19150306_art0012.txt | included=true | included =====
TESTING.

A system of grading can only be successfully carried out with the assistance of accurate testing. Inefficient testing will lead to confu- sion and largely defeat its own ends. Facility in testing can only be acquired by practice, and for this reason it is desirable that the operator should devote the greater part of his time to the work. Testing is therefore best uridertaken when there are large quantities of eggs to be dealt with, as, for example, at a Co-operative Egg Depot and in the local mar- ket. The test might take place where the eggs,! are purchased from the producers, who could then be pant on the basis of quality, and not on the present system of all=round prices. No greater stimulus could be given to the produc- tion of first-class eggs. Testing and grading are already leading features of the improve- ments in handling introduced by co-operative societies, and are also being adopted by better- class local traders, and accommodation should be provided for the, purpose iat ill local mar- kets If this were done retailers could then purchase with confidence from local collectors, who, by giving enhanced prices to reliable pro- ducers, would in their turn compel other poid• try-keepers to Om at the sartie standard. It is necessary to emphasise the necessity for grading and testing to be carried out. in such a way that the guarantee of quality can be ac- cepted without hesitation. The importance of this is well recognised in some Continental countiies.

The New Veils.

So far flowers form the trimming of four out of every five hats, and they are used as bou- quets, wreaths, or singly. *White velvet sailor hats have wre4tlis of daisies made of silk, in cOlours beautiful in their soft tints and delicate shadings, but utterly unlike the modest, natural flower with its white petals, and golden centre. Other wreaths are com Posed of tiny, almost fairy-like, flowers, and these are often placed :in a slanting position across the top of the hat to accord with the tilt necessary to the success of the small pork pie turban. This 1870 tilt, as it is called, has brought the veil of that period back again, but with .a slight difference of course. It now depends from the back of the hat, and reaches the shoulders, falling about ;the neck in. ratler full ,folds. These veils are in colours as well as in black, and match the straws of the hats they „accompany. They are necessarily of a verk fine Make,and have very little pattern (usually only a spot or two), their decorations being reserved for the borders. Sometimes these are quite deep and consist of a hem of moire silk, relieved here and there by means of a tiny flower.

===== 0002601_19150306_art0016.txt | included=true | included =====
Motor Fashions.

Every, day the number of women who own and drive their own meters increases, and each succeeding spring efforts are made, with more or less success ;to render motor millinery and coats a little less ugly and unbecoming. This year a very neat, close fitting cap, resembling a biretta, promises to be a great favourite. There are bennets also, but so far there is nothing distinctly novel in this connection. Coats and wraps are loose .fitting as always, and those modelled on Empire lines are decid- edly graceful, and in some instances even ele- gant. Ita"ny of the collars are cut to resemble a corner of a shawl in shape, and yokes, when used, are frequently quite fanciful. Sleeves Are still big and loose, with drooping armhole, but wrists are tight, in order `to exclude dust.

HINTS ON HEALTH. Q

Useful Information in Popular.

Language.

Sir Philip Sidney gave the following advice about health : "The rules of health and, long life are Moderate diet, open air, Easy labour, free from care." "Moderation produces clear, complexion."

Virtue and Health. A life of virtue is a life of health. Self- denial leads to self-development on higher planes. Patient battling against lower lust's ends in assured victory. To one man, and \to one only, is life worth living, and that man is he who resolves on nothing else than per- fection of body, niind, and soul.—" Great Thoughts."

• How To Go To Sleep. The following method is described as .one which is .almost certain to woo slumber with success: On going to bed you assume a com- fortable attitude in which every muscle is re- laxed, but not the attitude in which you are accustomed to go to sleep, though something resembling it. 'Every movement; coughing, yawning, is strictly repressed, especially the desire to turn over. The same attitude is maintained with- out change, ,constantly/ resisting the longing to move or turn over. .

As .a rule, by the end of fifteen or twenty minutes of this persistent maintenance of the same attitude you, will find yourself growing very drowsy, . and then, just as the desire, to turn over becomes .absolutely uncontrollable, you turn with the least possible effort and assume the position in which you habitually go to sleep, and itatoral sleep follows at' once. This method, it is claimed, seldopi fails.— "Exchange." Gumboils.

This is a condition of inflammation ending in an abscess about the root of a decayed tooth. ,A ,chill will often bring it about when there is a bad tooth and the health below par. Symptoms: One tooth becomes painful and seems a little raised above the rest, the pain often relieved by clenching the teeth together, though after a day or two the affected tooth becomes quite tender; a swelling next appears at the side of the tooth and in three or four days the pain subsides, or an abscess forms'and bursts generally between the gum and cheek, or it may be on the cheek, if there be a cavity in the' tooth it should be stopped with cotton- wool soaked in pure carbolic acid (keep-sit from touching the gums or inside of mouth), and if the pain and swelling still remain the tooth should be ,pulled. If the abscess requires lanc- ing it ,should be done inside between the cheek and gum, and never allowed to wait until it bursts on the outside, as an Unsightly scar will remain. Wash the mouth out with a suitable. mouth wash—Sanitas, one teaspoonful to a tumbler of boiled water, or Condy's fluid about same quantity. • ,

WHAT COULD SHE EXPECT?

Caller: " I find that house I've just rented from you is full of black beetles." Landlord: 'Well, at the rent you are paying what did you expect to find it full of--white mice?"

WHAT HE DID.

A little boy, of five was sinvited to , a party. The next day he was giving an account of the fun, and said that each of the little visitors )iiid given either a song, a ,recitation, or mush for the pleasure of the • rest. " Oh, poor little Jack !" Said :his mother. "How, very unfor- ,tunate you, could do nothing I" "Yes, I could, Mother," replied the young hopeful, "I stood up and said my prayers."

WOMAN'S WORLD.

Europe were, always fighting more or less, and peace was merely an interlude brought about by climatic, conditions, or the exhaustion ,of both, or arl the belligerents, marriage by proxy was by ni) means uncommon. The parents and guardians of children arranged marriages for them, and it frequently happened that as the youths, when still quite , young, departed to fight in distant lands, to carry out the con- tract at the pre-arranged date was impossible. Yet not to do so would have resulted in much inconvenience; hence 'the'- custom of marriage by proxy. The French Chamber has lately brought in a Bill, permitting French soldiers at the Front to whom leave is rarely granted, to marry in this way, but the rules and regu- lations to be observed are somewhat cumbrous and ini•onvenient.

CHAT and GOSSIP.

By MARJORIE.

Precautions In Royal Palaces. .

German aircraft have already made an at- tempt on Sandringham, and it is satisfactory , to know that precautions have been taken to preserve the priceless treasures of Windsor Castle from destruction by bombs". So perfect ,is the organisation to Meet the risks of fire, that every picture and every piece of tapestry can be ,removed from the walls in a few min- utes. The staff of firemen and workmen has been increased and extra drill is the ordcr of the day. Other treasures can be removed to a place of safety with equal rapidity, and every- thing Tiossible has .been :done to minimise the risk of destruction by hostile aircraft.. The same precautions, have been taken with regard to the treasures of ;Buckingham Palace; whilst the Crown Jewels in the Tower are specially protected from injury.

===== 0002601_19150306_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
Race Meetings.

Most people are: inclined to think that race meetings are out of place during the war. The buildings and stands at Epsom have been in use for military purposes, ,and the proposal' to disturb these arrangements in order, tp hold the Spring Meeting is not one that commends itself to the Duke of Portland, or .the public generally. Ascot will be, held as usual if noth- ing very untoward happens, but it' Will be shorn of all its customary. glories. The King and Queen will not entertain at Windsor, and there will be no Royal lirocession. Neither their Majesties nor any members. of the loyal Family are likely to attend 'races whilst the -war lasts. and the greater number of society women will be otherwise engaged than in indulging in mere pleasuring of this -kind. _ •

110111 E NOTES,

Profitable Recipes and Useful Suggestions.

A Girl's Looks.

How many girls realise that there is far more. attractiveness in a young girl or woman who is appropriately dressed 'at all times, whose hair has a well-brushed appearance, and whose com- plexion is clear, than in one, perhaps, with far more regular features, and. may be even a saucy dimple, but who doesn't really care what she wears or how she looks unless it be for some special occasion. The girl who only takes trouble on special occasions seldom looks really nice. Early rising, softhat there is enough time to have a good sponge down, if not a bath, and, also to dress leisurely afterwards, the, careful putting on of clothes, no matter how plainly' they may be made, and the regular brushing Of the hair, make a tremendous difference in one's ,appearance after a while. Ido not know, says a writer in "Farm and Home,". if you are aware of the fact that the passing of a silk handkefchief over the hair after it has been brushed will give it a nice glossy appearance. I cannot say why it does- this, but it is a cer- tain fact.

===== 0002601_19150306_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
Baked Smelts.

Wash thoroughly, dry in a cloth, arrange in a flat baking dish, after buttering both fish and dish. Season with salt and pepper, and cover with breadcrumbs. Place a 'piece of butter on each side, and bake twenty minutes. Garnish with parsley and lemon.—`/Mark Lane Express."

Spiced Mackerel. - Take one half-dozen small mackerel, nicely cleaned. Mix together one teaspoonful each of ,cinnamon, 'allspice, and cloves. Open the fish and sprinkle in the mixture; then sprinkle with salt and close tightly, place in an earthen dish, cover with vinegar, and bake one, hour in a moderate Oven.

===== 0002601_19150306_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
Treacle Pudding.

Make a nice paste by chopping together four tablespoonfuls of flour and half a teacupful of dripping. Mix it with cold water, roll it out on a toured board, rub a little dripping over a pie-dish, put a layer of paste, cover this with treacle, putting layers of paste and layers of treacle until the dish is quite full, with a piece of paste to cover the edges of the dish. Bake in a hot oven three-quarters of an hour.

Simple Puddings (quantities for one person). Ginger Pudding: Take three ounces bread- crumbs, an ounce of treacle warmed and melted, one ounce of butter, creamed, quarter teaspoon- ful ground ginger; mix with milk, and steam one and a half to two hours.

Barley and Apple Pudding: Wash two ounces of barley; soak over :tight; cotik in a saucepan two hours; pour into a buttered dish; add one or two sliced apples, a little sugar, and an ounce of butter, creamed. flake an hour._

Schools for Maimed Soldiers.

The French Government is • establishing national. schools in which disabled French soldiers may be trained to earn a living. The first of these institutions is in the Bois de 'Vincennes, in Paris, and here the • men are brought as soon as they; have finished their surgical and medical treatment. They are provided with appliances ,specially suited to their individual deficiencies, and are taught under healthy surroundings some trade or craYt. A Scottish hero • of the Boer. - War—Captain Towse—who lost his sight in that campaign, 'offers an example how • brace men may bear their misfortunes. He is doing all in. his power to lighten the darkness of our men ..who have been blinded in Belgium and France. He is now within sound of the guns, cheering the men by reading to them and • proving by his example that life still offers much worth hav- ing, even When so. precious ,a gift as sight has been lost

Stewed Codfish.

Take a thick piece of the salt 'fish; lay it in cold water for a few Minntes. Shred it in very small bits, put over the fire in a stewpan, with cold water; let it come to a boil, turn off the water, and add a Pint of rich Milk to the fish. Place again over the fire and boil slowly five minutes,, ddd a piece of butter, a little pepper, and a thickening of flour ill a little cold milk. Stew a little longer, and 'stir in a well-beaten egg.

THE ONE THAT WON.

By JAMES TABERNACLE.)

'1 would rOher live in the country than in the town, because, I think the air is so pure and healthy. There is none of the dirty black smoke in the atmosphere to kill the beautiful flowers and the vegetables, which is so common in the town on account of the number of works.

" In the country one feels ,the power of God's love to tnankind„ in providing. ,for our daily needs. What is more refreshing on a summer morning in the country to open the window and take a long breath of fresh air, then after- wards to have a good breakfast, then away on your bicycle past farmyards and fields—seeing the wonders of Nature?

"Of course there are disadvantages in the country. There are no shops to -make pur- ekases and very often you have to carry water. And there is no gas, which would be very strange to us. Then, again, the winter is very dreary; but it is pleasant to gather round the fireside and have a quiet chat, and then thank God for His doings."

FARM AND GARDEN.

THE MANURING OF CORN IN SPRING.

Autumn Wheat.

Land which has received 10 loads or.. More of dung per acre for wheat seldom needs further manuring in spring, and the same is generally true where this crop-- follows a well-manured potato or root 'Crop; or a good clover root. In view, however, of the exceptionally wet weather prevalent during the past few. Months, a litrge: amount Of soluble plant food must have been washed-tout, and this, coupled with the inability of plants to establish a deep root-hold in water- logged soil, will place the young wheat crop at a disadvant4ge when favourable weather sets in. A further consideration is the present high price of wheat. There is every likelihood, therefore, that suitable dressings of artificial manures this spring will be amply • repaid. • , The most important manurial element for wheat at this stage is nitrogen, which will, as as rule; be best applied in the form- of nitrate of soda. From 1 to 11- cwt. per acre,_ according to circumstances, should be given as soon as the risk Of severe frost is past. If more than 1 cwt. is applied, it is desirable to give the manure in two dressings at 'an interval of not less than a fortnight. The later dressing should usually be on by the first week in May in the Eastern Counties and South of England. If nitrate of soda is skilfully used as much as 2 ewt. per acre may often- be profitably applied. With a view to hastening maturity, and improving the civality of the straw and grain,- about 2 cwt. per acre,/ of superphosphate miglik be applied any ..time in March, where this is deemed. advisable.

===== 0002601_19150306_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
Spring Wheat.

- Unless the soil is. highly .fertile it will a's rule be desirable to apply some artificial. manure for spring wheat, with the two-fold object of encouraging growth and hastening maturity. A dressing of cwt. .to 1 cwt. of sulphate of am. monia and 2 or 3 cwt. of superphosphate per acre, will usually answer the purpose: The manure should be applied before the. final liar- rowing, or superphosphate may be applied when harrowing after sowing, .and 1 cwt. tell cwt. of nitrate of soda may be top-dressed in April. If the spring, is Wet, and the young wheat is yellow and backward toWardc the end of April, nitrate of soda will prove most useful.. •

===== 0002601_19150306_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
Oats.

:In the later ciistActs where the growing of spring wheat 'would be attended with risk, and in all districts known to grow oats satisfactorily, .farmers would be well advised to devote con- siderable attention to this crop this year. After pasture no manure Is usually necessary, but after most ether crops, it will probably 'pay well to apply some .manure unlis the land, is known to be in a high state of fertility. In most climates to 1 cwt. of sulphate of am- monia mixed with 2.0 r 3 cwt. of superphosphate and supplied at,seed•time, will usually give sat- isfactory results. In late-ripening districts, or on deep, productive land, superphosphate may be used, .either_alone Or in combination with a smaller quantity of sulphate of ammonia. On light land, particularly in the drier districts, or if in any circumstances it seems necessary to help a weak plant, a top dressing of 1 to 11 cwt. per acre of nitrate of soda may take the place of sulphate of ammonia. The application of nitrate of soda should not be too long delayed after the plant is up, or ripening will be re- tarded. It should usually be applied when the crop is 3in. to 4in. high.

===== 0002601_19150306_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
IRRIGATION

In the dairying .districts of the West of Scot- land liquid manure is used very successfully for the irrigation of meadows. Along with rain water from the roofs and the water used in washiug the byres daily, the liquid manure is run over the, nearest convenient sloping field. A few channels made with a plough are sufficient to direct the water and urine in different parts of the field, and with a Spade and a board the flow cart ,be altered from the main Channel as required. Liquid Manure which .at present runs into a ditch can be.utilised in this way by dam- ming the ditch by the side of a grass field and irrigating from the overflow. Very large crops of hay or grass for cutting are obtained in this way. It is to be noted that liquid manure alone is too concentrated for is,rigation, and must. be well dilated if rim over grass fields regularly. Another excellent method of utilising liquid manure is to apply it direct from a' liquid manure 4•ii rt. When used in this way it is again desirable that the liquid should not be too strong. t

LARGE SUPPLIES OF WHEAT.

The Board of _Agriculture and Fisheries have received the following information from the International Agricultural Institute : In countries in the Northern Hemisphere, for which there are data relating-to winter cereals, an increase is generally reported in .the areas sown compared with last year. esPecially in India and the United States, which show in- creases of 22. per cent. and 11 per cent. -respec- tively. Regarding crop conditions, although the season has been exceptionally wet .in many countries, no damage of notable importance is complained of as a rule at present. The good reports on the Southern Hemisphere wheat crops are confirmed, the production for the group comprising Argentina, Chili, Austra- lia, and New Zealand, being. estimated for 1914- 15 at 134,23.1.000 cwt., compared' with 126,246,000 cwt. in 1913-14, or an increase of 6.3 per cent. The 1914-15 production of cotton in. India is estimated at 17,67000 cwt., or 99.9 per cent. of last year's production. • • Steps are being taken by. the Government of India' to regulate' the export of their new wheat' crop. It is anticipated that after satisfying the needs of the Indian population there will be a large surplus available for export.

IN THE GARDEN.

PURPLE SPROUTING BROCCOLI.

. This is one of the best .and most reliable 'vegetables for spring use, for it is quite hardy and very often when the white kinds are cut off this escapes. ,It is to my mind one of the most delicious vegetables of all the'Bra.sica family. Some peoplt, may dislike the colour of this ,broccoli, but when once it is tried few will object to it, for it is of mild flavour, and one Of the tenderest vegetables in season .in spring. Though no novelty it is much neg- lected in gardens and shOuld receive more attent

FLOWERS FROM SEEDS.

Those who hare flower gardens and only a limited "amount of glass to keep during the winter the usual run of bedding plants, as

zonal pelargoniums, calceolarias, fuchsias,. aml such like, must make the most of plants that (:alt be easily raised from seeds. One reason. why there are so many failures with these itt that the sowing of the seeds is too' long ferred, very often until nearly the time the plants are required. The consequence of this,. delay is that the plants are rushed on in heat: and are weakly and drawn, the summer being half gone before they cover the beds. Sow early in ..March in a pit or greenhouse, and let them come along gently; then when the wea- ther improves they can be gradually hardened off, and when planting out time comes in May the young plants will be stocky and soon go away. Blue lobelia, petunias, -verbenas, tro- pieolums, asters, stocks, single • dahlias, gail- lardias, phlox Drummondi, and, many Other useful plants can all be raised in this way. If sown in pans or boxes and carefully pricked out under glass lights room is saved ,in the .greenhouse, as pot plants occupy so much space, and not nearly so many pelargoniums are needed. The bane of summer bedding is the lack of variety, but if annuals were used more largely greatet variety could be had with far less trouble in the way of storage through. the winter. With the above I should like to include snapdragons, of which the variety :in colour .is now very great. They can be had in separate colour,• which come very true.

FRUIT TREES IN .KITCHEN GARDENS. The old plan of mixing fruit and vegetables up together ends in both being unsatisfactory. because it, is at the best only a makeshift method, as it is quite impossible to grow two crops to perfection on the same piece of land. It is far better to devote one portion ,to fruit and the other to vegetables. As an instance of the folly of growing fruit trees and vegetables together we • remember many years ago in a North Country garden having seen two very large trees, one Blenheim Orange and the other na.wthornden, which had been planted in the. vegetable quarters. They so shaded the ground that no vegetables could be grown near them; in fact, the ground was practically • useless so' far as a bottom Crop, was concerned. Fruit trees-need all the food they can find in the soil for several yards areaind their base if they are to perfect good crops of 'fruit, reason the old wide-spreading standard tree is best given a place in the orchard. As, how- ever, many amateurs have insufficient space to grow fruit and vegetables apart, it fol- lows that they must fall back on some re- stricted form of training the old espalier form- coming in well for apples and pears. The, espalier can be grown on the margins of walks, and will bear freely if the trees can be allowed a sufficient area for their rooks and are well 110 Ur isbed. Next to these the hush trees worked on the • Paradise produce excellent crops if the trees when the fruit is swelling are liberally mulched with Some good rotten manure, and if the weather is dry given tre- quelit soakings of water, as the roots, being. near the surface, are very .apt to. suffer. ,

( :(11 'N( .ILLOR PEMBERTON'S CRITICISNI.

Councillor PEMBERTON subjected the Budget proposals to long criticism. lie began by say- ing that he had anticipated an increase in the rates, but he had not looked for such a de- crease in the revenue from the Gas Depart- ment, 'although, of course, as an ordinary business man, he had always reg4ded a decrease as possible, for they could no expect, year after year, to go on milking onVconcern to the serious extent that the gas concern had been milked. Councillor Wilkinson had told, them that he had been able, and that he had been very pleased .at being so able,- to :keep the, rates at Bs. for some length of time, but a point they ought seriously to consider was how had they been kept at that figure. They had been kept at that figure because the Council did not face the music as they ought to have done, but bad, instead, followed a policy of meeting their needs as they arose out of the various working balances.. A great many members of the Council, he asserted without the fear of contradiction, did not really under- stand the position; did not, for instance, un- derstand that if the Council had met their obligations out of the rates last year, the year before that, and three years ago, the rates would have been more than 8s: in the .2; indeed, last year alone they would have been more like Bs. 6d. Ito contended that they ought not to be in a position to depend upon taking such large amounts from working bal- ances, for such balances ought not to be so large and if they were not allowed to be so large it would, very often, tend to greater economy on the. part of the Couneil, • Many times during the last four years he had raised his voice against the practice was now con- demning, but it had been “like a voice crying in the wilderness."

One point which struck him very - forcibly was that •if they looked- back fourteen• years, viz., to 1901, they would ,find that they collected. in rates 4:57,090. Last year the total .collected was 4:105,181—an enormous increase; 'indeed, it was almost double. Let them just think of it! Almost double the expenditure in the short space of fourteen years! He did not see where they were getting value for such an enormous increase; although he, knew that there was a great deal of difference of opinion on that they are getting value for such an enormous increase, they were still not facing the music; not facing it despite the fact that they were proposing to levy a Ps. ,4d. rate. POOR INCOME FROM MUNICIPAL UNDERTAKINGS:

Councillor Pemberton went on. to refer to the water undertaking, with its capital of half a Million of money.. Here they had a great undertaking, a monopoly, with power to supply twenty-five townships, and yet, during the whole time, he had been connected with the Council lie had only known of one sum of £5OO being •abrogated front this monopoly to the relief of the rates, whilst, oil the other hand, the charges they. made for their Water were amongst the highest an England. In the water, the electricity and the tramway undertakings they had stink a combined capital of something like ,t 700,0011 and they were all monopolies with no competition to. facc or to r fear,' and yet they only paid that year the magnificent —(laughter)----sum of rt. 3,000 towards the rates. "There's fine business for you!"Aleclaimed the Councillor with emphasis. " That's the way these concerns—these monopolies—are man- aged. •Why, if they only paid a paltry two per cent, they would fetch in a sum of £14,000 per annum. When the Waterworks, belonged to a private company they used to ,pay a divi- dend of ev_per cent., and then. there was a reserve- fund, and pipes were laid. and every- thing. else dime out of capital. I am not con- tent. with this sort of thing myself; and it astonishes. Me that the people of Warrington tolerate it, because they are, practically, share- holders ii these concerns. They are the most patient and enduring people I have ever come across in the whole course of My life. ..It has beeriasaid" that even a worm will turn, but I don't know What would make the ratepayers of Warrington turn. (Laughter.) -- The Water Committee have an income of ,00,000 a year, and yet this year they have unit a farthing to give to the rates; and yet they eontemplate going on with fresh schemes which will, cost more money. It's a wonder to me whe're all the 1/1011- got'S SOWING THE WINO, REAPING THE' WHIRLWIND.

The speaker next turned his attention to education finance, and said that in 1905, the year after the present Education - Act. came into operation, they had an. education estimate of £10,408, whilst this year they were being asked to find A:29,000. In ten years the expenditure had been doubled, despite the fact that they had fewer children in the schools by 200. And yet- with an estimate of ,t 29.000 they were not facing the music, but were going to take £B5O out of the 'balances at their command. He was astounded, the more so, as they must not think that the present; expenditure was only a flash in the pan. They had 'sown the wind and they would reap the whirlwind. For they could hot for ever go on spending the thousands and thousands of pounds they had been doing. There was Warrington Bridge. for instance. According to the estiniates that was going to. cost £lO,OOO, and .t 14,000 was the lowest tender, but on the recommendrion of the late Mr. Webster, the engineer, the cost was increased to .R 22,000 0(14. 'Well, what had it actually cost, although not yet finished? The cost up to that night was -t.23,222 10s. Bd., and he was told that that did not include the 'retaining balance.

Councillor IlExsunn: It is ,tl.OOO. The 31-IYOR : [ can tell Councillor Pemberton that it is A:1,400.

Continuing, Councillor Pemberton said that at the rate things were going on, nobody could tell what the bridge was going to cost, adding that its might have been built for £15.000, but for unnecessary and reckless extravagance, which was hanging a millstone,round the necks of the future generation. What: would the public out- side say, sooner or ,kater. for they were not all fools. After referring to the disparity iu the amounts paid by the ratepayers and contributed by the Government in respect of education, and contending that Out. of the £60,000 found for education locally the ratepayers ought to pay something ,like £17,000 and the Government „gum() the 'Councillor went on to say that the people ought to kick and kick effeetiTely against IC for the Government would never effect an alteration, whilst the people took it as they did. There were practical men in Warrington; men who knew what business was, and the wonder to him Was that they did not grumble more than ,they did. Still, the time would come—he had 413,0 doubt in his own mind that it would collie— when, if the Conntil did not practise more economy, the ratepayers would take some means to force the members :to dew so. Look at the un- necessary expenditure; look at that eighty-foot ,wide biridge, for instance. when a sixty-root bridge7Woild have sufficed; and the extra and needless cost involved in so great a width. Westminster Bridge was only 85 feet wide, and why didn't they in Warrington go in for a 90 feet bridge, and beat the London one (Laugh- ter.) GAS COMMITTEE EXCLUSIVENESS. 'Another .thing, and an important one, having a bearing on this question of economy, he hair

only found out for a certainty that day—one could keep learning every day in that Corpora- tion—and that was that the Treasurer's depart- ment had nothing at all to ido with the financial condition of the Gas Committee. The Gas Committee kept its own books and finances, and all the Treasurer had to do was to pay the ,sinking fund and interest, and that was all he knew about the matter. Did the Council wonder at things being what they were? No firm would tolerate the pettifogging jealoustes which existed between committees on that Corporation. "That's our work," said one com- mittee to another, "and you must not know anything about it, and if you try to we shall resent it." If that sort of thing were in a Comic opera on the stage it would be one of the fun- niest things ever seen, but it was not in a comic opera; it was far too serious a matter for that. They should certainly link the financial side of the work of the Gas Committee up with the work of the Treasurer's department, SO that the committee's business could be conducted on the same lines as a sound private firm would con- duct its business. The Council spent too much money on -unimportant improvements in the back of the town; improvements that were of no earthly use. They improved streets that were only used by about ten carts a day. That would be all very well if Alderman Bennett's beautiful dream of the day when the profit earning con- cerns would be doing so, well that there would be no need for any rates; came true, and all the fifteen-foot streets Could then be made into beautiful boulevards—(laughter)—but unfortun- ately that dream had not yet come true, and it was high time they gave over dreaming and came to realities. In conclusion, Councillor Pemberton said that if he had his way he would not again return to that Chamber a single man who sat there and did not raise his yoide against the way the ratepayers' money was being spent; it was high time ;the ratepayers " outed " all such, and. insisted that their representatives , should get down to serious business.

ALDERMAN BENNETT'S ANALYSIS.

Alderman BENNErr said that he had no doubt Councillor Pemberton's.speech would meet with the approval Of those wile only looked on the surface- of things and disregarded altogether the benefits which were to accrue from the money spent. lie was bound to say quite frankly that when he heard the rates were going to be increased to such a great extent he was very much disappointed. He thought they would be higher, but he had not .the slightest idea that they would be, anything like so much as was proposed. But when one came to analyse the matter, the explanation seemed to be tolerably simple. PREFERS LOW RATES TO CHEAP GAS. He was one of tho-e who had continually argued in 'favour of getting considerable profits from municipal undertakings, such as, gas, water and electricity. lie had always thought it was a mistaken policy, having regard to the 'projeas they had to face, to be continnally crying out for reductions in the price of gas and for other cheap means of popularity; which were bound to come back in the long run' and hit them in the face. In .his opinion, low rates were much more important to a town like, War- rington, particularly from the point of view of those who wished to introduce new industries, than cheap gas. We could not eat our cake and have it. Last year the gas undertaking. produced' £M000; this year it will only pro- duce .5E4,000. There was ,t9,000' accounted for right away. Personally, he would prefer to continue to pay a bit more for gas rather than have this tremendous increase. in the rates. It would be easy to mako, dividends out of the municipal -undertakings, such as the gas and water concerns, if they could put the public off with any kind of supply, but instead of being niggardly in that direction they preferred to give a good supply at a reasonable rate. The people could not have it both ways. An ordin- ary company could not let the ratepayets have the gas at the price they were new paying; their price would be something near double what the Council were charging, as they would see when he recalled that the price used to be, under the •compapy, 3s. Gd. Per thousand, and was now only 2s. his own view was that they ought to strike a happy Medium, but, at the same time, they should be able to put by con- siderable sums to provide against such a heavy advance in the rates. A point which advocates of private enterprise seemed to overlook, was that none of the great limited companies had to pay' off their capital by a-series of fixed annual instalments, but the Corporation was obliged to do that before contributions in relief of rates could bo.rnade. HEALTHY,SCHOOLS COST' MONEY. - --

Referring to the increased cost of education, one wondered when the end of these repeated advances would come. Other questions which suggested themselves, were: " Are we getting value for the money expended?"—(A Voice: "No.")—and "I -low are we going to stop' these rises?" The Only practical suggestion he tad heard from Councillor Pemberton was that the children should continue to. be suffocated in inadequate premises—that the, Poor little souls should go to schools in which there was no opportunity of breathing fresh air, where they were crowded almoSt like rats in a hole, where the prospects of a healthy life and sanitary surroundings were utterly hopeless. By. revert- ing to the method of putting two children where they now accommodated cue, they might reduce the cost considerably, but was it worth while? Ile would rather pay an extra three- pence, and even more, for the purpose of .rear- ing a healthy, intelligent race. There were, no doubt, many points in the policy of the Edu- cation . Committee that were open to genuine criticism, but on the whole he 'thought the committee were fulfilling their duty well .and wisely and that the next generation would be most grateful to them. There; was one point they all agreed Upoll, and that was that the proportion- of the cost of education received from the Government was by no means enough, and, but for the war, they were 'given to un- derstand, that the -matter would have been . . readjusted. TOWN PROUD OF THE BRIDGE.

Councillor Pembei lon. had had a grand. fling at the bridge. (Laughter.) If there was any- thing that everybody else was satisfied about, they could always be pretty sure that Coun- cillor. Pemberton would butt into it like a bull inta a china shop. (Laughter.)- Ire (Alderman Bennett) did not think that even the long- suffering ratepayers, if canvassed, would show a preference for such a bridge as Councillor Pemberton had hinted at, in Place of the hand- some one they had now got, for he was sure that the overwhelming majority of them were proud of the new bridge and were very pleased to paY for it. (Hear, hear.), WHERE THE POOR GAIN. • - 7

Councillor Pembertoit talked as if the poor were the people who were mostly by any steps taken to iminove the town, but he (Alderman Bennett) took the diametrically opposite view. , It did not matter a bit to him whether -Warrington was attractive or other- wise, because he conk! go and live away from the town, but it tlid matt....r to ,i)eople who .had to live in the place and .spend the whole of their lives in it; it did matter to :them -whether the place was decent or not. (Hear, hear.) Practically, the great bulk of the expenditure proposed that night was to Imake the town more healthy and mdre fit to live in for the great /mass of the population. It was the 70,000 he thought about more than the. few who were able to take themselves outside the town. The worst friend the working man could have, was the.. man who, from false notions of economy, - sought to limit the facili- ties necessary for the welfare of the working man himself—facilities such as parks, baths, libraries and sanitation.

'there was no eaithly reason why municipal affairs should not be managed just as well as private firms, and he thought that in the main the Corporation of Warrington did manage the town's affairs.,extreraely well, and at a cost .which, after all, only ,worked .out at a penny per head of the population per day. When they came to consider what they got in the shape of comfort, health and 'convenience for so small a sum, he did not think the people

had much cause for complaint, for it was cer- tainly not a very high price. (Hear, hear.)

COUNCILLOR ROBERTS: "1 TOLD YOU SO."

Councillor ROBERTS said that twelve months ago a number of the members of the Gas Com- mittee resisted, but unsuccessfully, the final attempt which was made to reduce the price of gas.

"I remember," he continued, "telling the Council that I thought the probability was that the loss of contribution in aid of the rates consequent upon that policy, would be a serious matter. It is a serious matter. (Hear, hear.) It is not a pleasing thing to take up the role of 'I told you so,' but I am justified much earlier than I Anticipated by the results which have followed that mistake. There is an in- equitable side of gas reduction, of 'Which mem- bers of the Council need to be continually re- minded. Our limits of supply extend consider- ably outside the borough—(hear, hear)—and every reduction in the price of gas is to be accorded, not simply to the ratepayers, but to people outside Warrington, who are not rate- payers. The result is that you have this large element of gas-consuming people who get the benefit of any distribution of profit on an under- taking solely financed by the ratepayOrs of War- rington, • without n ,either responsibilities or contribution in the way of rates at all. (Hear, hear.) We have had- our cake, and we have not got' it now, and this reduction in profits, mean- ing practically a deprivation in rate aid of .CB,OOO, is a very serious matter indeed. I hope we have reached the end of any gas rcauctions, and I am only afraid that the Council may have to sanction • an increase, in the cost of gas." Councillor Roberts went on to say that he had listened to a great deal of Councillor Pember- ton's speech; and it was much on the lines that his speeches usually followed--95 per cent. -of growl and hardly 5 per cent. of real valuable contribution towards the solution Of a very serious position. SICK AND TIRED OF GROWLS.

It was all very well to be continually raking up the past, but irhe would apply his abilities to a 'suggestion of a practical nature by means of which the future could be better protected against the errors of the past, then .every one of them—even he (Councillor Roberts); though it would require a great effort on his part—(laugh- ter)—would welcome Councillor Pemberton's criticisms; but he must confess to being sick and tired of growls, without any practical sug- gestion whatever. The increase of rates meant an additional .1500 a year to the firm he repre7 seated. A matter of £lO a week was not picked up in the ..street, but the firm had not lost their confidence in the administrative capacity of the Council. They believed the town was getting and would get value for its money, and so they were prepared to pay. The allegations made against the Education Committee • were extremely unjust, and by no means represented the facts. We were suffering to-day, and would continue to suffer for years, owing to the laxity and the very small value placed upon educa- tional facilities in years gone by. The Education Committee had to ask fen nearly £3,000 mere than last year, and that I.epreseltited 1.76 d, in the .1 on the rates. No less than half of that was due to the increase of teachers' salaries, made 11P Pf .4500 for the staffing of OakwO-od- avenue School, .1200 in respect of the patriotic recognition by the Council of the noble duties which .many members of the staff were perform- ing in the military service of the CroNirn, and £BOO as the normal increase of salaries according to the scale sanctioned by the Council. Refer- ence had been made to the proportion of the cost of education paid respectively by the Gov- ernment and from the. local rates. The propor- timi was: Government 54 per cent., rates 44 per cent., other sources 2 per cent He would only be .too delighted if by any means whatever the proportion could be altered, so that the Govern- ment bore more of the cost and the ratepayers less, but he was afraid there was no probability of such an alteration at the present time. The expense per child in Warrington as a direct charge on the rates of the town as 31s. id. per annum. In Manchester. it, was 655. _lld, per an- num; in Bootle, as. Bd.; Liverpool, 655. 6d.; Southport, 535. 3d.; Blackpool, 565. 9d. Out of 16 towns in Lancashire, there were 11) consider- ably higher than Warrington, and only four below it. That, he thought, was not a- very un- fortunate position to be in. Only that week they had received from the Board of Education an intimation that seven of the .schools in War- rington were overcrowded, and further reduc- tions of grants were threatened unless more accommodation was provided. For the present they were to be mulcted in a small fine for the purpose of reminding them of their duties.

THE MONEY SPENT ON EDUCATION.

If • the town declined to make further provi- sion, the only alternative would be that the ,Board of Education would step into the shoes of the local authority and carry on the business in their own way. There seemed to be no Way out; he wished there were, and that he could see it. Some 80 or 90 per cent. or the money devoted to education in Warrington was spent upon the children of the working classes. He did not begrudge any expenditure that would enable qualified brains to get to any position. That. policy had guided the Education Com- mittee for some time; they believed in no aris- tocracy except the aristocracy of brains. "If ever," concluded Councillor Roberts, "there was a time when it was necessary that there should be no stinting in, regard to that provision which the, future will make so absolutely essential in our brain equipment, that time is now. When this unhappy war is over—as we all hope it soon will be—God only. knows what the position of many matters in which we are interested will be. But Ido feel that the same spirit of magni- fieent patriotism which has enabled us to face. the most' fiendish. 'combination of adverse cir: cumstances and conditions the world has ever seen, and which will carry us safely and tri- umphantly through the . struggle, will also, I firmly believe, animate us when we come to con- sider not how little but how mueh we can do for the children whom God has given to us in order that they may maintain untarnished every possible reputation which justly belongs to this magnificent Empire of ours." (Ap- plause.) NO UNNECESSARY NEW WORK. Councillor HENEMALL submitted that the.truth in this matter was to bp found somewhere be- tween the pessimism of Councillor Pemberton and the optimism of Alderman Bennett, , and he did net profess that he was going to find it out. The rate, of course, was a bit of a stag- gerer, but there were certain' elements about it hi regard to which .the Council we:p utterly helpless, as for instance . the increase in the Government requirements' in the Matter of the cubic space per ,scholar, which had increased 25 per cent. since 1904, when it was eight cubic feet., per scholar, whilst now it was ten. Whe- ther they got value for the money they spent on eduction might be a matter. of opinion, but he believd that the Education Committee got the best value they Could. The point he wished to emphasise with regard to municipal work generally was that , policy determined finance. ,Were not the Council even now embarking upon policies that would involve them or 'their successors in the same strain of lamentation that had been heard that night?. The contem: plated Sankey-street 'improvement was one of those things, which they ought not to touch at present. agar, hear.) The Chancellor of the Exchequer, at the beginning of the war, ad- vised all. municipalities to be rigidly economi- cal, and he (the speaker) egntended that, with- in. reas,on, they ought not to embark upon anything to 'which they were not already abso- lutely committed.' They ought, not, to touch the shadow of ,a shade of a fringe of a policy that had in it the possibility of an increase of rates, whatever its potential advantages might be, but, ought to follow a course of rigid economy.

WOULD DEFY THE GOVERNMENT.

The DEPUTY-MAYOII (Councillor Joseph) said he would like to add his lament to the chorus of lamentations that had been uttered with regard to the increase in the rates. Coun- cillor Roberts had stated that there was no way Out in the matterof the increaeed cost of edu-

cation, but be had a plan, crude though it might be, and that was to let the Government take the_matter into their own hands and see if they could make a better business of. it. They would then see how unprofitable it was for .the committee to have to work it on the grant which `was allowed to them, or on any- thing like it, and how the burden that was being borne by the townspeople was gradually increasing, in spite of the excellent manage- ment" .of the committee, in whom he had the utmost confidence. "We are all faultless, of course," said the Doctor, amid laughter, "but I -do not think we shall ever have the full benefit of real, good, sound business tactics in the management of our affairs until party politics are abolished and we have a business Council just as we have a business. Government at the present time. This unfortunate and lamentable war has afforded us a powerful illus- tration of how the parties in the Imperial Parliament can. do their work in a pleasant and business manner, and ,it certainly vindi- cates to the full my own advocacy of the aboli- tion of .party. politics in this Council. They are the one bugbear, or, at all events, one big obstacle to, our progress, which, I hope, we shall see abolished in the near future."

Councillor ARCHER suggested the appointment of a sub-committee to go into the estimates of the various committees and see whether any- 'thing could be referred ~back for further con- sideration, and added that, in his opinion, the condition of the sewers was a disgrace to the town, and had something to do with the abnor- mally large number of patients at present in the infectious diseases hospital. . • . CRITICS' ANNUAL "BEANO."

Alderman TINNION reminded the Council that a reduction of rates could not be effected by talk. If so, there had been enough of it there that night to wipe out all their expenditure and give them a , dividend on it. (Laughter.) "I am • sick of it," said the. Alderman; "I do not know to whom certain members think they have been talking, but if they imagine that they have been gulling other members of the Council they are sadly mistaken, whilst if they think that they have been gulling the public of Warring- ton they are equally sadly mistaken." What is the use of them-coming and talking absolute rot and, nonsense here now. They have had their share in nearly every expense. (VoiceS : "No. no !") Well, if they haven't had, they ought to have had, for they have been neglecting their duties. ("Oh, oh," and laughter.) They ought to take their share in the responsibility for this rate. ' This is not the time to come preaching economy; the proper time to preach that is dur- ing the twelve months in which the 'expendi- ture has been incurred. • (Voices: "We have.") It Would be much better if those members gave us a little more fact, instead of talking out of their hats as they have done to-night. (Laugh- ter.) If I had thought that there was going to be so much rubbish talked here to-night I should have taken the trouble to get out some facts concerning a good many schemes that some of these gentlemen have started and which, to my mind, were altogether u?inecessary and which, certainly, have been costly and have not been beneficial to the ratepayers. In season and out of season, instead of waiting for this annual beano,' these critics should see that the , mqney, every penny of it, is properly and care- fully expended. lam here to-night to say, and, if neeesiary, to argue. the point, with anybody here, or outside, that the expenditure of this Council is not on the reckless lines that some of the members would endeavour to persuade the ratepayers is the case. All .the business of this Corporation is very carefully considered, and I do not admit that we have made mistakes, for I ! think the Warrington tatepaYer is getting good value for h money?, COUNCILLORS WHO CRY "STINKING FISH !"

Continuing, Alderman Tinnion challenged Councillor Archer to prove that the existing sewers were responsible for the number of cases of infectious disease in the borough; nay, more, he contended that the sewers of Warrington, considering the physical difficulties under which the town laboured, were better than

those in any other town in Lancashire. (Cries of "No, no!") Really, the number of men who came there to cry stinking fish got on one's nerves. Much had been said about the price of gas, Yitt,he said, and said it deliber- ately, though agreeing that they did not want to make a profit out of it, that if they did not raise the price of gas during the next twelve months they would be making their gas below cost price. Moreover, instead of making a pro- fit out Of the'gaS supplied to the outside dis- tricts they would be actually giving them so much a thousand feet fel- taking it. • ge failed. to see that that was sound business. • MIgTAK E. -

• At this juncture-there*as sO much behind Alderman Tinnion between Councillor Pemberton and Councillor Lashford that Alderman Tinnion ceased speaking, and, in reply to- the Mayor, he (turning 'so as to face Councillor Gough, sitting near Councillor Pem- berton) said "It is the same thing again." At this juncture there was an interruption, and, turning round, •Alderman Tinnion re- marked : "That infernal nuisance, Councillor Samuel Gough, has been at it all the evening." Couecillor Gores (surprised): Why,l never spoke! You have turned the gas on. (Laugh- ter.) Continuing, Alderman TINNION went on to argue that the Gas Committee would be losing £B,OOO to £lO,OOO if they chArged the Same prices during the ensuinglnancial year as they had charged 'this. Gas was a comthodity that could • not be made se cheap by individuals as it could by a Corporation; and people ought to pay a fair price for it. He wanted them to remember that if they reduced the price of gas in Warrington they were giving something that could be distributed among the people of War- rington to the people of Stockton Heath, people whom lie had not the least sympathy for, for he could not consider the man who got his bread and butter in Warrington and went and spent his money outside the town. (Hear, hear.) '

Councillor WILKINSON briefly replied to the debate, pointing out that the estimates. of all the committees were practically the same as last year, and that the fault lay in the fact that they would be minus £9,000 from the Gas DepaKment, and that they had to find .P. 3,000 more for education. On the top of that, the difference in wages occasioned by men who had gone to the Front, wa5:5.3,500, ,and they were thinking Of giving all the workmen a war bonus on aceount. of the high prices of food- stuffs: There was no Wilful waste of public money. Councillor LASIWORD : Oh, yes!

The. resolution was thin put and carried, only Councillors Pemberton and tashford voting against it.

Improvement and general sewers rates of 4s. and 4d. in the rt, respectively, were then passed without dismission.

COUNCILLOR OSBORNE AND PROFIT-EARNING.

Councillor OSBORNE said that the dominating factors in the increased rate were the decreased contribution from the Gasworks, and .the in- crease in the education rate. Why did not the Council face the facts with regard to the con- tribution from the gas undertaking? If they wanted the gas consumer to pay .the rates, why,. did they not say so? 11.1oA of the con- sumers were resident in the borough; they were, for the most 6art, either the industrial users of power gas, which was supplied at very little above cost price, or the occupants of the thousands of small homes which could not afford electric light or where gas was used for cooking purposes, ,often through the penny-in- the-slot meter. Were these people to be taxed in order to make the rates lower for the more opulent ratepayers, or for the shareholders of limited companies, who might live out of town? If so, let the Council face the matter fairly and squarely and raise the price of gas. (Hear, hear.) But if they took that course, why not also increase the charges for , electricity and water and also the tram fares, until they got all the money they needed for the rates from the profit-earning departments? It seemed to him that it was not what the various under- takings were producing which had caused the heavier rates, but the abnormal expenditure they had been called upon to face. He was one of those who advocated cheap gas, because he believed that to supply commodities at the cheapest possible prices was the only equitable way in municipal government. (Hear, hear.) But unfortunately the more money they derived from the gas undertaking, the more extravagance there would be in other depart- Ments.

===== 0002601_19150306_art0050.txt | included=true | included =====
WHERE THE MONEY GOES.

If the ratepayer S wanted to know where a lot of the money was likely to go, they had only to take a walk down Buttermarket-street and re- gard the operations there as a sample of what would probably take place in Sankey-street. Property was taken down, rateable value was destroyed, and sites were left unproductive. Then again, if they had wide bridges they must pay for them? If the rates could only be kept down by charging abnormal prices for commo- dities, such a method Would be a reflection upon the integrity and honour of any representative of the municipality. It was unfair for mem- bers to say with reference to the reduction in the p,rice of gas, "I told you so" When rates went up, while no mention was made of the sudden intervention of war conditions and the way in which the gas department had been, handicapped by the increased- cost of fuel and other abnormal conditions. (Hear, hear.) So long as lie remained on the Council he would continue to advocate the supplying of every commodity which the,Couneil had to ,offer at an equitable price, fair alike to the user and to the ratepayer, and, if the rates could only be kept down by charging abnormal prices for those commodities, then he thought it was a refleetion on the honour and integrity of the members.

DELIGHTED WIT'II TIIE BRIDGE. Councillor HAWTHORN expressed himself as de- lighted with the appearance of Warrington Bridge, and thought it would have been a mis- take to have had a narrow structure, • such as Councillor Pemberton thought would have sufficed, for the whole of the expense they were now incurring was due to the fact that their forefathers had built a narrow bridge. Councillor LASHFORD thought that in view of the fact that the water-department did not give a single penny to the rates, the time was not ripe for embarking on the projected new water scheme, which, he said, might cost anything from 420.006 to £25,000.
