THE EXAMINER,

SATURDAY, MARCH 6, 1915.

111611 RATES.

The Council on Tuesday -night con- firmed the recommendation of the Finance. Committee making the rates Os.. /id. in the z.te, instead of Bs. in the an increase, as we said last, week, that constitutes a record for W.arring- ton. Bad as was the .increase, the discussion which took place was, we think, infinitely worse. There was much carping criticism- and some ill- feeling, but very little help in the way of showihg how the rates might have been kept' down on this occasion, or may be reduced or prevented from rising still further in the future. • We fear the real spirit of the debate,,, from ,one point of view, was revealed in the suggestion by Councillor PEMBERTON that the most pressing need is a full report, 'of all that is said in all the committees and at all the meetings of the Council. Such a record would I give a Councillor an opportunity of looking up all his adversary had said in order to produce it. against him at a convenient moment. :That might. lead to more talk—which cannot be said -to be a pressing, necessity when one looks at the condensed report of the. Budget debate On Page 3. But it would scarcely lead to economy; the. cost of employing shorthand- writers upon one of the biggest tasks that was ever set the profession would add con- siderably to the rates. , The common-sense of the Council as a whole recognised that the increase. was inevitable. The contribution' from the gas profits and other muni- cipal .undertakings fell away to the extent of £0,500, and the cost of education is to go up, this year by £3,000. 'There are other extraordinary charges due to the war, and the whole _unfortunate increase is accounted for. Some of it might have been avoided if the price of gas had pot been re- duced • but when th 6 reduction was made it. was not foreseen that the cost of production would be increased- by war. We cannot cry over spilt. milk; 'but we must feel concerned at. the statement of Aldgrinari 'TINNION that if we continue fo sell gas at the pre- sent price we shall lose £B,OOO or £lO,OOO in the coming year, upon .the Cost basis that is bound to rule. And what will the rates be then? As to education, we are paying, at the most inopportune time,-, it is true, for the shortcomings of those who in peace time neglected, to provide the town with schools 'in which children could assemble in something like decent sanitary conditions. We are now giving the children a fair chance,' and though we are .providing them free education in . addition to Suitable schools, and an adequate staff of teachers, they only cost the rates .eightpence per head her week the. year through. Something was said about the cost of :Warrington Bridge. Councillor PEMBERTON complained that ' the cost, had gone up from £1.1,000, the lowest ,tender, 'to £23;000. But it is' hardly fair to make such a comparison, as, the latter figure includes all ,the expendi- ture that.AhaS followed upon the mak- ing of the Bridge, such as £2;000 for altering mains and cables, and was not, of course, in the tender to which he referred. He also said the Bridge should have been narrower. But five busy thoroughfares Converge,upOn the 'Bridge, and most people, who use it will say it is not an inch to wide,

===== 0002601_19150306_art0063.txt | included=true | included =====
DRINK IN WAR TIME.

Does More Damage than all the German

Submarines.

MR. LLOYD GEORGE'S HINT.

"There are some, I am sorry to say, who shirk their duty in this great emergeiwy. I hear of workmen in armaments works who refuse to work a full week's work for the nation's need. What is the reason? They are a minority. The vast majority belong to a class we can depend upon. The others are a minority. But, you must remember, a small minority of workmen can throw a whole works out of gear. What is the reason? Sometimes it is one thing, sometimes it is another, but let. us be perfectly candid. It is mostly the lure Of the drink. They refuse to work full time, and when they return their strength and efficiency are .impaired by the way in which they pave •spent their leisure. Drink ig doing us more damage in the war than all the Ger- man submarines put together.

• "We are armed with full powers .for the Defence. of 'Hie Realm. We are approaching it, I do not mind telling you, for the moment, not from the point of view of people who have been considering this as a social prolrlem—we are approaching it purely from the point of view of these works. We. have.gat great powers to deal with drink and we mean to use them. We shall use them- in a spirit of moderation, we shall use them discreetly, we shall use them wisely, but we shall use them quite fearlessly, and I have no doubt that, as the country's needs demand it,..the.country will support our action and will allow no indulgence of that kind to interfere with its prospects in this terrible war which has been thrust upon us."—Mr. Lloyd George.

a time when we need to strain every nerve and to 'put forth our whole national strength.

COUNTING THig LOSS. The productiveness of the labour of adult wage-earners, male and female, may be safely taken at 255. per week—it is mOre when we .consider profits as well as wages. .Taking the number of these workers as 10,000,000, and assuming their effectiveness reduced by one- tenth through heavy drinking, there is a loss of ,t 1,250,000 a week. When to this the ex- penses involved in dealing with the products of excessive Oinking, the shortening of life in the working period which might be termed loss of life capital, are added, the weekly loss might, be safely taken at £2,000,000 ,a week. Germany will have, to be far more active, and far more successful in destroying our merchant ships, before the weekly loss caused can approximate to this sum. If by curtailment of facilities, the waste of money, and the drain on physical efficiency could be reduced even 5 per cent., the step should be taken without delay.

The question is not primarily one in which those who object to the consumption of intoxi- cants in any form or any quantity are alone concerned. The 'war' has shifted the question on to an entirely different footing, and those who do not hold extreme views on this ques- tion are as ready as those who do, to support restrictions, even if these restrictions involve a slight inconvenience to themselves. If, as Mr. Lloyd George has said, "the _government use their power in a spirit of moderation, discreetly and wisely," there is no doubt the country will support them in their action, even if, as Mr. George puts it, they are used fearlessly.

===== 0002601_19150306_art0064.txt | included=true | included =====
RUSSIA'S EXAMPLE.

A Great All-rotmd Advantage.

A large employer writes : Mr. Lloyd George's statement at Bangor on Sunday on the drink question and what our Allies have done was timely. He said, and said truly, that "drink is doing more damage in the war than all the German submarines put together." From the social standpoint, the greatest change resultant from the war is the prohibi- tion over the whole Russian Empire of the sale of alcohol In the form of vodka. We have had various reports as to the results of this prohibi- tion, and that which carries most weight 1, the recent utterances of the Russian Finance Min- ister, M. Bark, who tells us that his "anticipa- tion of the result has been greatly exceeded by reality, the solvency of the peasants has been raised very considerably by this law, and the beneficent' operation of this edict continues to make itself felt. progressively." .He assures us that "the productivity of every class of work- man in Russia, whether we examine thole en- gaged in agricultural or industrial pursuits, has already augmented by from 30 to 50 per cent." These are startling statements, espe- cially coming from one who speaks with the authority of a finance , minister. Had we not the facts confirmed by others we should be dis- posed to consider them exaggeration, even although they emanate from such an authority. We are further informed there has been an un- precedented decrease in the criminal ,returns, and that a great change is spoken of having come over Russian village life; the children are better clothed than ever before, and the savings bank deposits have gone up with great rapidity. There was lodged in the Russian savings bank in September two and a half millions sterling more than in the corresponding month of the previous year. The consumption of spirits per head of the population of Russia is 1.17 gallons at the English strength; in the ITnited King- dom it is .71 gallons, taking Scotland alone the consumption is 1.41 gallons. It was certainly a bold step to take to cut off at one stroke, at a time when a Vast amount of money was re- quired for war, a source of revenue amount- ing to £80,000,000 per annum. Not only did ,Russia prohibit the sale of spirits, but greatly restricted the sale of beer and wine.

===== 0002601_19150306_art0065.txt | included=true | included =====
WE MUST MOVE SLOWLY

, In spite of the universal approval with which this autocratic action has been met, it is not one that we should like to see adopted in this country. Reforms are more likely to be perma- nent when their growth is natural, and it will be very surprising if in Russia there is not somewhat of a reattion. Still, we think more might have been done in England, and by general - consent, in reducing the facilities for • the, consumption of intoxicants, and in pre- venting excessive drinking. A general la-, ap- plicable alike to all places, would have been much better than the one passed, placing the onus of the recommendation to restrict upon the chief constable. We should have then felt that the sacrifice was general, that it was national; and the inconvenience (in no case very serious) was made by all for the general good. The application of this law has been erratic, not so much regulated by the amount of abuse of alcohol in any particular place, but largely by the attitude which the chid of the police held to the question, influenced-no doubt by the ideas of the majority in power. Hence in towns where the authorities took a lenient view of the abuses, of the use of ,alcoliol the chief of police has, rarely moved, while in localities where the majority of those in power were advanced along the line of general curtailment - the chief has advised earlier closing. The responsibility ought 'never to be put upon the chief constable. Selecting those towns where earlier closing has been, enforced, it is difficult to see why this has been done, if we are to judge the action by the condition of other towns, say, for example, Warrington, where no restrictions were recom- mended. The plan adopted of closing at all all sorts of hours, and at some places opening at, different hours, was bad, inasmuch as it pro- vides by comparison a legitimate grievance both to the licensees and their clients. Why should some in a particular town be made to sacrifice their profit or convenience, when no similar demand was made on the trade and public in neighbouring towns? In a matter of this sort when sacrifice is enforced by law it should be universal. The result has not" breh satisfactory, and the application of the law most erratic, and not always applied a.: intendetliy the Legisla- ture.

===== 0002601_19150306_art0066.txt | included=true | included =====
FOOLISH SPENDING.

There is no doubt that a great many find beverages pleasant, in which the alcoholic prin- ciple is the vital part; and, in the majority of cases, little harm results. But, on the other hand, there are many heavy drinkers who take these beverages in excess, and spend their money foolishly. As Ramsay Macdonald says, "At the best Of times spending foolishly is folly, but at the worst of times spending foolishly is • criminal." It is this criminal spending which requires checking, and a general curtailment by 'closing earlier than eleven would not place upon those who take intoxiCants in moderation any appreciable in- convenience. Our nation is engaged in a death grapple for its very existence, and every unit, both at home and at the Front, is called upon to conserve vital, force and material resources. The call has gone out to the men and women of the British Empire at home and overseas for sacrifice and for service. The 'demand is for a nation inspired by high purpose, determined, calm and strung, willing to 'put aside everything that 'lessens the strength of bady;, steadiness of nerve and power of controlled will. r

The Russian Finance Minister estimates the augmented working power of the Russian people through the prohibition of vodka at from 30 to 50 per cent. Even if we estimate that excessive drinking Yeduces Our industrilal efficiency only 10 per eet4. the loss is one wo.eannot afford at

===== 0002601_19150306_art0067.txt | included=true | included =====
DRINK AND WORKERS

Output Reduced Appreciably By

Indulgence.

One of the Heads of a . large manufacturing firm in Warrington writes : _ _ _

The wise and courageous 'manner in which Mr. Lloyd George tackled this most important question aft Bangor on Sunday fias given the greatest possible satisfaction to all true pat- riots, and especially to those whose „association with our industrial world is intimate.

Long before anybody dreamt of the present war it was an accepted fact that week-end "boozing" was productive of infinite miscnief, and it does not need the wisdom of Solomon to realise that a man given to indulgence, in strong drink during the better part of Satur- day up to late in the evening, with a further series of " nips " on Sunday, cannot possibly be in first-class working trim on Monday morn- .„.. ing.

It is well known that apart from the physi- cally unfit condition of the men who thus in- dulge, but who manage to turn up on a Mon- day as *al—a condition from which they do not completely recover. until nearly the middle of the succeeding week, and which,reduces the value and quality .of their work for 'Mondays and Tuesdays by quite an appreciable percen- tag—there' are a number of others who lose time to a considerable extent, and thus reduce considerably the possible output of the works wh•re they are employed.

===== 0002601_19150306_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
INDIFFERENCE IN FACE OF A GIGANTIC PERIL.

Apply these facts to the existing situation, and what do we find? Our !country needs the largest nossible Output of the munitions of war to enable the splendid new armies to give effect to their training and patriotism by bringing the war to a successful and early finish. Our country is facing a crisis in her history more grave and pregnant with the possibilities of disaster than ever happened before. - At least two millions of our own best and bravest sons are risking their lives in this gigantic effort to maintain • inviolate the free- dom and the happiness: of the British .people.! What words can be strong enough to condemn ! the action of those who, for the sake of gross ; indulgence in a depraved habit, are so indif- ferent to the lives and happiness of their fel- lows that they are prepared to imperil even. the safety !of , the -Empire, and, in any case, and at least; to postpone the victory of our Armies because they deliberately render them- selves unfit to do their share (safely sheltered from all risks) in providing our fighting men with the materials necessary and even vital to their success? Surely such gross and selfish indulgence in this hour of national peril must be stamped out at all costs. If, however, the action of these self-indul- gent and unpatriotic men is beneath contempt, what shall .be said of "The Trade"- which. fattens on such vileness? Prom one end of the country to the other the Press has borne uni- versal testimony to the chorus of approval with which the great .Chancellor's • words of warning have been received, and it is certain. that any measure of restriction which the Gov- ernment consider necessary for reducing the possibilities for drunkenness will be warmly 'welcomed and .cordially • endorsed. "The Trade" will grumble and threaten—it will also blatantly proclaim aloud the Patriot- ism of its members. If Patriotism,- rightly understood, implies individual self-sacrifice. for the national good, then indeed "The Trade" will, and does, make a sorry show, for in every instance when the slightest attempt is made to prevent drinking on the part of our troops it is vigorously opposed by "The Trade," and the most contemptible motives are not infre- quently attributed to those who on the advice ! of those most competent to judge, act in ac- cordance with such advice and ,put "The ; Trade" under partial restriction. The opposition to such restrictions is fos- tered solely by the Brewing and Licensed Traders, who, in the words of a deceased hut extremely erratic cleric, would 'rather see "England free - (to get drunk) than England sober." The mistaken kindness of those who treat the soldier and thus endanger his fitness is deserving of condemnation equally with that of the publican and the brewer, who profit by what compels in part the soldiers' inefficiency. It cannot be endured that England shall lag behind her Allies as to the manner in which this danger to our country shall be fa ed and overcome.

===== 0002601_19150306_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
INDUSTRIAL UNREST.

The Settlement .of Disputes Iftegarding

War WOO..

Mr. W. Peter Rylands presided at the monthly meeting Of the Central Executive Committee of the Employers'. Parliamentary 'Association held in Manchester, at 'which it was resolved that the committee "views with satisfaction the concurrence of His Majesty's Government, in the recommendation of the committee appointed to inquire and report on the question of production in, ergineering and shipbuilding establishMents, with a View to providing a tribunal for the' settlement of dis- putes between employers and workpeople, in establishments. engaged in Government work. The meeting, • however, noting that His Majesty's Government propose to appoint the committee itself as a tribunal for dealing with such disputes, record the 'opinion that the Industrial Council would- prove to be a more suitable tribunal, having in the ,first instance been created for an analogous purpose, and including repvesentatives of both employers and workmen, its decisions would be likely to cOmmarid ihe, unhesitating confidence of both interests affgeted."-. .

===== 0002601_19150306_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE HUN'S HANDS.

Warringtonian's Experiences as a

Prisoner of War.

STORY OF GERMAN BRUTALITY.

SPECIAL TO "THE EXAMINER." Among Warrington's gallant sons who have fought and suffered in the great war is Bom- bardier George Though, who last week returned to his home in Wakefield-street. Greatly to his regret his soldiering days are now over, for he has lost his left leg in the service of his King and country, a fact which has cut short what bade fair to be a most successful Army career, for the gallant Bombardier had obtained promo- tion from the rank of ordinary gunner in the Royal Field Artillery at an exceptionally early age, being yet but little niore than twenty years of age.

HELPED BY PLUCKY COMRADES. It was as long ago as the 23rd of August that Bombardier BrOugh was wounded. On that day, he told an " Examiner " interviewer, his, bat- tery went into action at about the dinner hour, and fought steadily throughout the afternoon anc(gvening., It was about 6 o'clock that he was hit*, shrapnel shell bursting chise to his gun, and two pieces of it catching him in the left knee, one in the right toe and three in the back. Needless to say, he was suffering severely, and as he was in imminent peril of being hit again, two plucky Jerseymen, who were serving with him at the gun, and who bear the typically French name of Le Maistre, carried him, with great gallantry, to a safer place. In fact, they took him into a wood,/ and put him under a tree, so that he could rest his back against the trunk. Even there, however, he- was by no ,means out of further•danger, for ever and anon shells .came screeching through the trees, -and one of them lopped off branches from the very tree against which he reclined. Later on Bom- bardier Brough had a companion in misfortune, a private in the Gay Gordons being brought into the wood with one of his legs practically cut off.

TAKEN PRISONER BY THE GERMANS. As soon as it could be done with safety, Bom- bardier Brough artd the Highlander were car- ried to a private house at 'Ryon, just" outside Mons There, they received every 'attention possible, and there they remained for three days,' until the house, was captured by the Ger- mans, and they were taken as prisoners ,to the military hospital at Mons, then in the hands of the Germans. In this hospital the Bombar- dier remained up to a little over a .fortnight ago, when an exchange of prisoners took place, .and he and ;other seriously damaged British warriors were exchanged for a like number of captured Germans. Whilst in the, hospital at Mons, Bombardier Brough was attended by Belgian doctors and nursed by Belgian women, the Germans merely mounting guard over the hospital, save an officer paid a periodic visit and ordered the drafting into Germany of such of the wounded as were sufficiently recovered.

===== 0002601_19150306_art0071.txt | included=true | included =====
BELGIAN LADIES KIND TO HURT

TOMMiES."

Unknown.to the Germans, the Belgian nurses were most kind to the wounded British " Tommies," smuggling in extra fare for their delectation, and, generally,' doing all in their power to make theta as comfortable as possible. Once or twice the hospital. clean' ran out of rations, and at the best of times the fare pro- vided was hardly what one would have con- sidered suitable for invalids. A specimen day's menu was as follows

Breakfast: A pint of coffee and, occasionally, one round of brown bread.

Dinner: Potatoes and thin soup

Tea: Potatoes.

After leaving the hospital and prior to set- ting sail for home, Bombardier Brough had to spend three days with the Germans in Liege, and if it had not been for the kindness of the Belgian women of that town be and his com- panions would have been half-starved, as in their condition; they found it practically im- possible to eat the rough fare provided by the Germans, which mainly consisted of black bread, "as hard as boards," save when, mixed with horse beans, it was made into an anything but appetising soup.

GERMAN' SOLDIERS "ON THE CADGE." "The Germans," the Bombardier said, "were not cruel to us, probably because they remem- bered that we were' marked out as exchange, prisoners' and feared reprisals might be directed against their own men captured by the Britishl Still, one or two of the German sol- diers had a nasty knack of coming up to us and demanding a share of anything we might have, and we dare not refuse to give it them. When the time came to send us to England we were placed in a respectable hospital train and were given some fine soup, and on the whole we were treated rather well on the journey." Some of, the German wounded who were lying in the hospital at Mona were very decent fel- lows, said Bombardier Brough, and he got ." proper chummy" with one of them; a very nice fellow, who declared that if it rested with him and Many of his comrades the war would not last a month longer. Prior to the war this particular German had been in a good way of business, and he had been through no fewer than sixteen battles and had been wounded

GERMANS NOT ANXIOUS TO GO BACK.

Ag a rule, the wounded Germans displayed no desire to go back into the fighting line; indeed, on the days their own army surgeons came round they would keep to their beds and adopt all kinds of ruses to dec,eive the medico into extending their stay in hospital. Lying in a bed near to that occupied by Bom- bardier Brough was a German suffering from shrapnel, wounds in the hip and the knee, wounds of Such a nature as to make one of his legs inches shorter than the other. One day 'a German doctor came to the hospital looking for men to make up a party for active service again, aml he asked the or fellow "Can you walk?" The German replied that he could not, whereupon the doctor said "You would soon walk if I had an orderly with a bayonet with me!" and ordered the man to prepare to be sent back.,

===== 0002601_19150306_art0072.txt | included=true | included =====
TOLD OFF TO KILL A WOUNDED BRITISHER.

One of the wounded English prisoners told Bombardier 'Brough a shocking story of ei er - man barbarity, The 'prisoner in ,question formed one of a party of nineteen who were surrounded by the Germans and cut up. When further resistance was impossible the soldier, who had had two of his fingers blown off, put his hand up and called the attention of the officer in charge of the Germans, to his condi- tion. Thereupon the officer struck him across the neck with the fiat of his sword and knocked -him down, and then told a private off to kill him. The private gave the wonnded man two thrusts with his bayonet, the weapon going clean through him, just below the heart. He was then left for dead, but was subse- quently found to be alive 'and was taken into hospital.

===== 0002601_19150306_art0073.txt | included=true | included =====
"YOU ENGLISH PIGS; YOU KILLED MY SON."

During one of his periodic visits to 'the hos- pital at Mons, a German officer, who, like most of his kind, had a good command of our tongue, observed: "When we got to Belgium we had to speak the beastly Flemish language! When we take France we shall have to speak French, and when we get to England I sup- pose we shall have to speak your tongue.' "No!" observed one of the English patients sotto voce, "it's then you wake up!" On another occasion a German Staff officer visited the hospital and ,upon :the Wounded English prisoners being pointed out to. him he observed in English: "You English pigs; yon,Atilled my son."

Bombardier Brough never tires of singing the praises of the Belgian ladies who tended him when he was lying` helpless and nursed him back to health arid ' strength,

PUBLIC AMUSEMENTS.

RE %I. PLAYS AT , THE ROl 114 COURT.

"The Walls of Jericho" is a brilliant play— a telling play for those intellectually capable of being told; a play that sets a-vibrant all the chords that the artist-dramatist is ex- pected to play upon; but it is no play for the groundlings in matters dramatic, for it boasts no red-nosed comedians—save the mark !—and no mock-heroics that compel cheers or tears from the "gods' and there isn't a single spot of ber-r-lud abqut it, nor yet the slightest trace of melodra'matic villainy. It is a real play, and not' a series of nauseating spasms. fluctuating between the mawkishly sentimental and the revoltingly bloodthirsty, and it is sad commentary that in these days A-.D., and after goodness-only-knows how many millions of pounds have been spent in popular education, it still remains difficult—in this part of the country, at any rate—to gather together an appreciative audience for this high-class fare; as difficult as it is to get a houseful to become enthusiastic over the only .really great drama- tic-poet Of. whom the land can boast. The discriminating playgoer Will' revel in "The Walls of , Jericho," with its delicious epigrams, pretty phrases and clever situations; the undiscriminating may not revel in it, but, equally, he will not be able to prevent it tak- ing a very high place among contemporary dramatic literature, for, ,added to its purely literary worth, it is a play ,that pulsates with human interest; .because, after all, the story of , the butterfly-wife , wedded to the serious, indomitable man—who assayed the meretricious allurements of, so-called " society " whilst carving out empire in the "back blocks" of the Antipodes and . found them mainly com- posed of dross—is an intensely human story.

- The piece eceives excellent treatment at -the hands of a most excellent and Conscientious little company, each part being capably and carefully taken; with the result that the "tout ensemble" is every whit as satisfactory as one could possibly expect. Last night the company played The Lion and the Mouse "—another big London success—and, again, their repre- sentation was adequate. To-night they repeat "The Walls of Jericho," and, if you have a latent taste for the higher drama, and have not already seen, the piece; you should make a point of, not missing the performance. Lancashire Play Next Week.

For next week lovers of drama are promised a big treat in the shape of a visit .of Mr. E. Vivian Edmonds and his company in "The Maid of the Mill," a play dealing with Lanca- shire life.

HIPPODROME.

This week's very fine vaudeville menu includes Alfred Woods and his talented company in the brilliant dramatic scena, "Saint and Sinner." Charles Hanbury and company score a huge suc- cess with "His Last. Night Out," an excru- ciatingly funny farcical conceit, though one does not quite realise the fine humour of it all until the end. The Three Stewarts give a marvellous exhibition of head and hand balanc- ing, and Celina Bebe earns hearty applause with her violin and xylophone solos. The Dorrimer Trio are great favourites in their re- fined vocal act, which includes a number of patriotic ballads; whilst Tom Purslow proves to be a burlesque operatic comedian of the first order, fairly "bringing the house down." A fine number of "The Ga,umont Graphic" is also shown.

Jefferson) paid a tribute to the sacrifice the inside and outside staffs had made in providing men and assistance of various kinds for the wax. "The total amount paid," he continued, "in claims by your company is over £10,000,000. You will be interested to learn that, notwith- standing the claims arising out of the war, our mortality experience was more favourable last year than for many years past, pointing, I hope and believe, to a greater care in the selection of business outside, and, let us hope, also point- ing to a higher standard of living and to better conditions amongst the great mass of the people for whom we cater. (Hear, hear.) The results of the year's transactions has been an excess of income over expenditure of £261,844, exclu- sive of capital paid up." A WARNING TO CYCLISTS.

The success of the Raleigh bicycle, now by far the niost popular of any machine in the world, has naturally been followed by many imitations, and we are asked by the Raleigh Company to particularly warn the public against the num- ber of these, now upon the market. The characteristic features of the Raleigh. particularly such items as the tubular fork crown and other details, have been closely copied, consequently the company ask all buyers to see that the name " Raleigh " actually ap- pears on the trarlsfer, as in some cases even the trade mark has been closely imitated. The fact that there are now something like half a million Raleigh bicycles on the road has natur- ally led purveyors of shoddy bicycles to imitate the famous all-steel production, and in this con- nection foreign wanufacturers have not been behind-hand. Some of -/ the German products which were sent to our colonies to compete with the Raleigh were obvious imitations, although. usually so badly done that they could deceive no one. However, the warning to the' public will probably' be useful, because naturally the buyer of, the all-steel bicycle wishes to make sure that he or she is gettirig a real,. genuine

Corporation War Bonuses. At a special meeting held on Thursday the General Purposes Committee decided to grant a war bonus to the employes of the Corpora- tion. Married men earning wages ranging from £1 to .{:1 10s. per week will each receive a rise ' of Ss. weekly. Married men who earn from .11. 10s. to it will get 2s. weekly. and all tn- married employes will be paid an extra is. per week. The bonus will be withheld ;frem an man who loses' more than "a quarter per week, and will, of course, cease on the termination of the war. No increase of wages granted since the commencement of the war will be taken into account. in fixing the bonus, which will only be payable on the rate of wages prior to the war. At the monthly meeting of the 'Council on Tuesday evening Alderman Timikin referred to this .matter of the bonus, and asked to be allowed- to make it clear that -he repudiated, as strongly as he possibly could, any suggeS-' tion—and certainly the suggestion had been made—that he had been endeavouring .to scotch the suggestion to grant a war bonus.

The Question of Redundancy or

WHITE HART, MARKET-PLACE.

Application was made, for the renewal of the licence of this house.

The Chief Constable said the premises (an old' house) consisted of a smokeroom, small bar or snug. There was a room where dinners were supplied and also a billiard-room. About two barrels per week would cover the business in beer, except that there was a fair bottle trade and a moderate one in spirits. It was the only licensed house in the neighbourhood which catered for food for those who attended the market.

Mr. Evans, from the Borough Surveyor's office, said that within 220 yards there were 34 licences, equal to one licence for 67.35 of the population. Mr. Greaves Lord, on behalf of the owners of the house, Messrs. Greenall, Whitley and Co., said the Bench had not simply to looleat the mere amount of intoxicants sold, for this was a. house that people used not merely for drink, but also for food. BATHS HOTEL. -

Mr. Browne appeared to support the renewal of the licence of the Baths Hotel, Sankey-street and Golborne-street. He had no objections to the figures 'which had been got out. The Chief Constable entered the witness-box and was about to speak as to the trade done, when

Mr. Browne asked that the particulars might be put in in confidence by the Chief handing them up to the Bench. The. Chairman said that was asking them to conduct the case in camera. He had never heard such, a request in, his 21 years' experi- ence. It was a public court. The Chief Constable then stated that the weekly sales at the house were 141- barrels, 52 dozen bottle, and 12 gallons of spirits. Mr. 'Browne said Viat the Baths Hotel was one of only three frie houses in Warrington, and was also one of the best conducted places of its kind in Warrington. WHITE HART, SANKEY-STREET. ,

Mr. Lord applied for the renewal of the lie\enee on behalf of Messrs. Greenall, Whitley and Co.

It was .a house, said the Chief Constable, which was doing a trade of twelve barrels weekly, besides 40 dozen bottles.

Mr. Lord said it was not a question of re- dundancy. Where such a large trade was done, it became a question of differentiation. There were three houses near by and the one eaßable of dealing with an increase of trade was the White Hart.

BANDS IN THE PARK.

Mayor Offers to Defray the Cost

of Them.

The members: of the Town Council were in most loquacious mood on Tuesday evening, and among other things n which they found material for discussion was a suggestion sub- mitted by the Town Hall, Parks and Baths Committee, relating to a proposal to purchase a new carpet for the Council .Chamber at a cost of XlO2 75., and a criticism of the cora- mittee's action in not including among their items of estimated expenditure for the ensuing year the sum of £7O usually paid to bands for summer concerts in the Park, the war being the reason given for dropping the addition to the amenities of the social life of the town.

Councillor OSBORNE said be was very much against the deletion of the estimate for the, bands. He was not saying it for the purpose of gaining notoriety, but it gave him no pleasure to think that his committee proposed to take away that X7O worth of enjoyment from the working classes. —He believed that if they did start economising they should start at- the right end, and so far as he was personally con- cerned he would support any proposal to pay the bands as heretofore. It was Attie enough the bandsmen got—about Is. per Wad per per- formance.

The DEPUTY MAYOR (Councillor Joseph) said he was as pond of music as anybody. but he really thought that it would only be doing the

proper thing to dispense with the band concerts in such serious days as these.

MAYOR'S SPORTING OFFER.

The MAYon said that though these be serious times, he failed to see why the band perform- ances should be dispensed with; in fact, he thought them more necessary than ever whilst the people had such thoughts on their minds. Ile contended that they ought to have the con- certs, and he would see that the bands were there "as usual," even if he had to pay for them himself. (Applause.) And the Council could have the credit of providing them! He had been going to ask for an increased grant for the \ bands, but he realised that the present was no time to do so, but. next year, all being v(ell, he would have pleasure in proposing a bigger allowance. Ultimately Councillor OSBORNE withdrew the proposal re the purchasing of the carpet for the Council Chamber, and the discussion ter- minated.

KNOTSFORD LIBERALS.

Mr. P. M. Oliver and the Outlawed Nation.

Mr P. M. Oliver, the Liberal candidate for the Knutsfoyd Division, speaking at the annual meeting of the Liberal Assoelation last Satur- day, said that the Liberal creed knew only one justification for war other than self-defence. It was stated in 1862 by. Richard Cobden in a speech delivered in Manchester. The nation which has been a party to a general system of international law," said Cobden, "becomes the outlaw of all nations if it breaks it towards one." Since those words were spoken there had grOwn up a system of international law,' the very corner-stone of Which was that the inde- pendence and integrity of neutral States should berespected. Translated. to fit the circum- stances of to-day Cobden's words would run:

"Germany, 'which has been a party to a general system of internationarlaw, has become the outlaw of all nations because it has broken that law towards .Belgium?" Cobden went on to say: "Such a nation would bring down upon it instantly the animosity, indeed the hostility, of all, the .other Powers who' were parties to the system. This is the guarantee that interna, tional -rules will be respected." This was the justification of Great Britain's intervention in the present war. The meeting elected' the following officers: President, Lord Sheffield; chairman, Mr. P. A. Haworth; hon. treasurer, Mr. H. P,. Greg; joint hon. secretaries, Mr. J. R. Tomlinson and Mr. J. Arnold Thompson. On the motion of Mr. S. If. %Allinson, sec- onded by Mr„ Frank Fox (Grappethall), the ar.Lual mort wa-;adcEte,d. t •

===== 0002601_19150306_art0097.txt | included=true | included =====
OUR TERRITORIALS AT THE FRONT

NEWS OF BATTLE."

First Warrington " Terrier "

To Be Wounded.

HET LEAVING TIIENCII,

. All doubt as to the Warrington Territorials haying been inaction is laid to rest by the receipt of a letter which came to hand. this week, conveying the information ,that Private Richard Smith had been wounded. This 'letter was received. by Mrs. Smith, of 60, Cartwright-street, Warrington,; from her husband, Private 'Richard Smith, who is in "A" ,Ceinpany, 4th South Lancashire Regiment. Re has ,been wounded, he says, and is now in hospital in France. "I got, hit on .March Ist," he writes,. "and I am pleased to •say I am qoing .cham- pion, so you must not upset yourself. I got ,a bullet through the back, and the doctor says I am a very lucky chap that things were not worse. I was one of the first to get hit in this. particular fight. I thought my time had come; being hit by a bullet is such a funny sensa- tion. Talk about 'stormed: at with shots and shell,' it is just .awful out here." The writer adds that he will pull through all right, and that he might be coming home to an English. hospital 'soon. He also says that he "got it" just as his company were geaing ready to leave the trenches for a rest.

===== 0002601_19150306_art0098.txt | included=true | included =====
NO vt‘ ORRY NEEDED.

Going Into Action Very Soon.

An officer, of the 4th South Lancashire Regi- ment writes under several dates prior to this week

I believe the trenches have improved a little owing to better weather. The Major ,is very good. He has supplied us with waders and periscopes. The devastatiOn is dreadful and the mud awful. Am feeling most delightfully clean and comfortable, having turned my

underclothing inside out this morning. We

look like a wild beast show in our skin coats. I doubt if they will be much use in the trenches. Once taken of they are difficult to catch again. It is difficult to write as every- one is talking and fooling about. P.S.—Our " Tommies " say they can get nothing to smoke, or, rather, that the stuff here is not Worth smoking. an you get some- body to send them some "Woodbines," as they seem to he first favourites.

===== 0002601_19150306_art0099.txt | included=true | included =====
A HOT "DO.'!

We are getting used to the sound of the guns, but there is a continuous row like heavy thunder. We are now between our own and the: guns, of the enemy. They are at it day and night, with intervals, of course, and the rifles crackle like blazes. They fire. more at night than day; we manage to sleep by covering our heads up. The other night there was a hot "(10" all night. I woke several times,, and each time the guns were hard at it; occasionally , the terrific boom of our heavy guns and centinuous crackle of rifles, then a machine gun .would chip in, then another quick-firing gun, all easily recognised by the sound. One of the most extraordinary sounds is the angry scream. of a shell. •-A few of us went to the top of a ,hill this afternoon to watch an artillery battle. We could see the shells exploding* qqite distinctly miles away. Some terrific guns are 'going into action ,ott our side: We 'had some fun this morning, practising witii grenades. We used dummies at first, but finished with the real thing. They are remarkably violent.

===== 0002601_19150306_art0100.txt | included=true | included =====
EVERYBODY MERRY.

The tales you hear are all piffle. Everyone is very merry and bright and in the best of health. The weather is not very grand; but the men are free from Colds. Tell those who worry that there is little risk on this job if one uses reasonable care, which I am impres- sing upon my men to do. We are in the 7th Infantry Brigade, 3rd Division, 2nd Army Corps. This may enable you to follow our movements in the Press. `•

P.S.—Did you do anything about the "Wood- bines"?

The "A" and “B" Companies go into the trenches on Monday, but our turn will come' in a few days. The din of the guns is becom- ing monotonous, but there are some very hefty guns just- behind, the explosions from which are terrific, and the concussion in the air gives one a knock on the head over half a mile away. The anti-air craft guns are kept pretty busy. It is great fun to watch them. Every one of us is in the best of health. I have never known less sickness in the Battalion. The weather is awfully severe, and the mud is appalling, and, to improve matters, there have been several inches of snow. We have to censor the men's letters so that nothing can leak out. The officers are put on their honour to disclose nothing that might be of use to the enemy. , I am afraid I shall not be able to write again for a few days, as I hear we are going into action very soon. P.S.—Glad to know the men will get their "Woodbines."

===== 0002601_19150306_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====
"BE AMONGST THE ' SCRAPPING '

SOON."

Warrington " Terriers " In "The Pink

Of Condition."

Mr. Rupert Jones, of 44, Baxter-street, War- rington, has received a cheety postcard from a friend who is . serving with the Warrington "Terriers" in France. The friend in ques- tion is Pte. Joseph V. Podmore, the son of Mr. W. Podmore, hairdresser, of Gaborne-street. Private Podmore was amongst the earliest of the Warrington Territorials to volunteer for active service, and he left the town to undergo -the necessary training with the first batch of "Terriers." In his postcard to Mr. Jones, he says: "Just a few lines to let you know that I am still keeping quite all right, and in the pink of condition. We shall be amongst the ' scrap- ping ' soon. We are billeted just behind the firing line. We are getting good food and are. well looked after."

===== 0002601_19150306_art0102.txt | included=true | included =====
" TERRIERS" IN TRAINING.

A Change of Quarters and Conditions.

The reserve battalion of the Warrington. Te:rritorials left their quarters on the West Coast 'of England for fresh quarters in the South on Monday night. "We had a great send-off," writes Private J. A. Llewellyn; "the majority of the population of the town lining the streets to have the last glance. It was like leaving home after having been there three months, and they did not like to part with us. The bugle band led us to the station and all the latest songs were sung. Our new training quarters are not the game as the old. We have to, rough it more. Ingtead Of a soft bed we have two blankets, great coat and the floor. The floor was a little hard for the first time, but as One gets used to such a bed it becomes natural." •

===== 0002601_19150306_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
OFFICER'S NARRATIVE.

Territorials Finest Troops At

The Front.

PARALYSING DESTRUCTKM

An officer in the Warrington Territorials, writing home from the Front, says : I have only just received letters addressed. to me at Tunbridge -Wells. Anything sent later will in due course reach me, though postal' de- liveries here are not so prompt and frequent as at home.

I wish you could see this place. It is hard, and not for me to describe it. First impres- sions are almost paralysing. Debris, dirt .and desolation—imagine the ‘appearance of Butter- market-street in the house-breakers' hands, and the disorder they make, and let your imagina-

tion recall a lot of what you have read about the depredations of the Germans, and you may picture something of what invasion and mechanical warfare can do, and be thankful that you are all at home spareii something of what the Belgians have suffered.' Although I cannot tell you where we are, still you may know-we are in the district where the British have done some of their best. work, and the district where they have suffered many casualties. One hardly realised in England the appalling loss of life there has been. and the strenuous work our'ul,en have performed.

consider the " Teiriers " are now the finest troops at the Front. At the present time we are at a point where the fighting all round is very severe.

===== 0002601_19150306_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
LIGHTING UP THE TRENCHES.

As I sit writing the big guns are thundering away. You cap hear quickening discharges of the artillery, and in fact see the flash; and

hear the shells whistling through- the air, and then finally burst somewhere in the vicinity of the Germans. The rifle fire ,is crackling away in the distance; it is. one shattering noise. This is night time; during the day time you see the German- shells roam through the air, bursting all around here within 1,000 yards of us, and as they explode the thud of the impact is stunning in its intensity. I can see, as I write, the lights which are periodically -shot up in the tießches; these are sent up by both sides in order to have a look at each other. There is much more rifle fire at night than during the day time. the great time being when trenches ,are relieved between six and eight in the evening. Trenches are relieved at present every 24 hours.

===== 0002601_19150306_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTONIANS UNDER FIRE.

All except myself in the list of names I give . . have already experienced their baptism of fire in the trenches, but have come, so far, through untouched. - - - .

• On Friday next the whole battalion takes its place. in the trenches complete as a unit. We take over a particular trench occupied at pres- ent by the . They are being relieved to- morrow by the , and then we take over from them.

Aeroplanes, when the air is clear, swarm in the air like flies. No sooner do they appear over one another's lines than the aircraft guns open fire. You see hundreds of shells fired, but never an aeroplarie comes down. The roads are, in places, knee deep in mud, and everywhere being re-made by the .military • authorities.

===== 0002601_19150306_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
A MASS OF TANGLES.

From what 1 now see it seems that the whole position has assumed a deadlock. Imagine two sides securely entrenched 100-150 yards apart, the country in between one mass of tangles and barbed wire, the artillery of either side being afraid to shell the 'trenches for fear of damn- ing their own men. They are Only able to shell the areas that contain the men in support, and the hostile artillery positions, and execute damage to the surrounding country about three miles on either side of the trenches. If you only saw the country and heard the descrip- tions you would understand some of the diffi- culty in the way of advance, and pronounce it almost impossible. We have the Bavarians up against us hero, and jolly good they are at their work.

It is now morning. I did not finish my letter last night; I went off to sleep. This morning we hear that the road we came along on Sunday has been shelled and is now impassable to wheeled traffic) We live in ape place obtainable. At present six of us are in a wooden hut, with corrugated iron roof. It is freezing hard, -and we do our best to keep ourselves warm huddled round such fire as we can keep going in a biscuit tin. Mud! We are up to the eyes. We have hard work always on, and shall have harder later, but everybody faces the hardships manfully, and undoubtedly the Battalion when it returns will have made a name for Warrington. The coinage is peculiar. You get Frenefh, Belgian and even German coins, and of course plenty of our own. It is all current.

===== 0002601_19150306_art0108.txt | included=true | included =====
ZONE, OF THE DEAD. Awful Picture of Ypres District.

"Eye-Witness," -then official descriptive writer with the British Forces, describing the Ypres and la Bassee districts, where the British troops are engaged, says The aspect of the narrow strip between the hostile lines in the woods round Ypres almost defies description. If the reader can imagine what a wood would look like with most Of the trees either felled altogether or half-gawn through and lying with their tops on the ground; if he can further imagine this weal standing in soft muddy clay in which every few yards there is a pit several, feet deep; and if hp can further picture to himself the whole of this iangle of dead vegetatibn, mud, and deep pits, heaped from end • to end with thousands of German corpses, the majority of whom have lain there since November, he will then gain some idea of the appearance of this awful zone of the dead that lies between us and the enemy.

From the time when the great attempt of the enemy to break their. way through to the sea was finally abandoned in the middle of Novembep,- no; serious :effort has been made to penetrate our • line,: nor has any great concen- tration of German troops taken place on our front, and such activity as they have displayed may be described as merely that of an active defensive.

In the last three and a half months the only heavy flghting has taken plate on the flanks Of our line, namely, in the La Bassee and Ypres areas.

All the efforts of the enemy have, .however, failed to advance his line in this area; he has merely occupied in two or three places short lengths of a trench. Our line runs round these points and close to them. The 9riginal attack on the 14th took place after fresh drafts had been received by the enemy, many of the men had never been in the trenches before, nor were they giVen warning that an advance was intended until ten minntes befOre. the time appointed .for the assault. This fact would ‘eem to show. some 'distrust of the new levies who were going under fire for the first time.

Tliis 'tendency of the enemy to lige new troops for an attack before they have lost their ardour by a long period in the trenches has been shown in the fighting near La Bassoc.

RALLY ROUND, BOYS!

Yotaing Bombardier's Call To AVarring-

ton Lads. In a repent letter to us, Bombardier P. Broad- hurst, of "A" Battery, R.F.A. (69th Brigade)- -who is well known in junior football circles in the town—describe § the 'training he has under- gone, and, up to tile time of writing, was still undergoing, in the South. ===== 0002601_19150306_art0111.txt | included=true | included =====
"IN THE PINK" AGAIN!

Frost-Bitten " Tommy " Who Is Ready For More Fighting. . Among the soldiers who have been invalided home from the Front, but, happily, now have fully recovered; is Pte. W. Gaffney, whose home is at Brooklands, Longford. Like his neighbour, Pte. Povey, Pte. Gaffney had finished his. time in the Army and his six years in the Reserve, but joinedvagain for the duration of the war. In the latter end of September he was drafted off to the firing line at Bomull, some 14 miles from Ypres, where he had 35 days in the trenches, including one stretch of 20 days with- out relief. He was in the Ist Battalion of the Cheshires, whose regiment suffered so much at Mons.

„His experience was such that he has no desire to go through it again, though now, to use his own phrase, he is "in the pink,” and will not shirk duty when called. upon again.

===== 0002601_19150306_art0112.txt | included=true | included =====
A Sad Task.

The roar of the cannon day and night, almost without ceasing, for so prolonged a period, says Pte. Gaffney, got on the nerves of the men, mid many were the comrades who fell by his side and, whom it was his painful duty to assist in burying, when opportunity presented itself. Despite all its drawbacks. Pte. Gaffney has got a good word to say for life in the trenches. Food was good and plentiful; and the needs of the men were most carefully attended to. That' was why he had no hesitation about going back, It was while.in'the trenches that Pte. Gaffney fell a victim to frost-bitten feet, and on Decem- ber , 4th he was sent home, arriving ten days later. He has made satisfactory progress *o wards recovery, and is now ready for further service. His experience included plenty of night attacks.

===== 0002601_19150306_art0113.txt | included=true | included =====
"HEAD DOWN, HEART UP!" Local Private's Summary 01 Life In

The Trenches.

FIVE FIGHTERS FROM ONE FAMILY.

"Well, now we are in France, and not far from the firing line. We are waiting for the order to go to the trenches to help the —, who have just come from India. We are so close that we can hear the big guns fired, and see the gashes from them. . . . Well, it won't be long before we are in the firing line—and I hope that we will come back safe." So writes Pte. Stanley Corbett, of the 4th South Lancashire Regiment, to his parents—Mr. and Mrs. George Corbett, of the Old Warps, Victoria Park, Warrington. Mr. Corbett is keeper of the park, and an ex-member of the Borough Police Force. He 11.11 four sons and a sou i..-law serving with t' e colours. Fought the Boxers.

_ Of these four sons, Corporal George Henry Corbett (33), is the eldest, and he went out to the Front about three weeks ago to serve as a dispatch rider. The corporal's home is in Edge- worth-street, and in peace times he is a police constable, belonging to the .mounted police; thus he is well practised in the management of horses. Although he only enlisted in the Royal Horse Artillery a week or two ago, he had had considerable previous military experience, for he served two and a half years in China during the terrible Boxer risings. During that time the English military police were serving along with the German and French troops, and many friendships sprung up between the men of these different nationalities. ..Curiously enough, the English and Germans were then very great friends, and between them practically no squabbles took place—as in the case of the more excitable French. Of course, on the whole they all got on very well together, and the cor- pdral says that when he meets some of his old German friends—as he thinks he will—it will be difficult to regard them las enemies. Three More Sons Serving.

Mr.. Corbett's second son is Private James Corbett (24), of the 'Lancashire Hussars—now stationed in the South. This private enlisted at the start of the war in his old regiment, and is now in training, preparatory to leaving for the front. In civilian life he was a barman in one of Messrs. Peter Walker & Son's "houses," and his home is in Dickenson-street, off Bat- tersby-lane. Ernest Cothett (21), the third son, left his work on. • Messrs. Crosfield's box-makintY machines in October. and enlisted in the 4th Sou& Lancashire Regiment. He then left the Old Warps for the South, where he has been in training. "Not What Papers Say!" _

In the same Month the hone also lot Stanley (19)—an apprentice to Mr. Daniel Johnson, hamper maker. He became a private in the same regiment as his brother Ernest, and (as will be seen from the letter quoted above) has just arrived in the war zone., In another recent letter—passed, by the way, by Captain 0. Lyon Whittle, son of Warrington's Town Clerk—the private says: "I will be lucky if 1 see home again. But never mind lam doing my duty. This is a rotten place! It is nothing like Eng- land, for it looks like a broken-down shanty. We have heard nothing about peace out here, for we hear . different things from what the paper says. We get the news from the men who have been in the trenches, and these men think that the war will be over in May. I hope it is—not for my own sake, .but for the sake of the people. . . . "It is funny," he adds, "that I, the youngest of the boys, am in the firing-line first. Well, all I have to do is to keep my head 'down and my heart up. . . ." I• A Fifth Fighter in the Family. _ _

Sergeant Charles- Brierley, of the Lancashire Hussars, is Mr. Corbett's son-in-law, and he is now with the troops in training "down South." By trade he is a butcher. and "served his time" with Mr. Benj. Cross, Corporation-street. The sergeant's home is in Nora-street. Like his brother-in-law James, he had previously served in " Gerard's Troop" (Lancashire Hussars), and was promoted to the rank of sergeant whilst in Camp at Knowsley. "Single Men Should Go."

COOKING BY CANDLE.

How " Tommy Gets A Hot Dinner kr

The Trenches.'

Pte. A. Povey, of the Cheshire Regiment, whose home is at West View-terrace, Longford, who was shot through the loft arm on khrist- mai Day, some four miles from Ypres, is now sufficiently recovered to enable him to rejoin his regiment on March 15th, when -his prolonged leave expires. He was sent _home on account of the gunshot wound, and, hag it not been for his, subsequently suffering from rheumatism, ho woullf ha-ve been back with Ma regiment a fovt- night ag% - _Hit While Repairing a Shuttered Trench.

It appears from his statement that just before Christmas- the Germans poured 831 ate fifty shells. into the Bridal' trenches and completely shat- tered. them. It was whilst engaged in repairing the trenches during the night thatt Pte. Povey was shot through the arm.

Pte. Povey jnined the special reserves six years ago, and his time expired in October last. He had some sensational experiences during his comparlttiVery short stay of five weeks in the, trenches. " Sic was well," he told atm repre- sentative, "so long as you did not pop your head up tooliigh. Ir you. did show it too much there was always the danger of having daylight put through it." lie was surprised, the gallant private added, to meet with "Jimmy" Riley and Fred Wade; both Warrington men; while at the base hospital in France. "Jimmy" was shot through the body two days before Pte. Povey, and was sent home. \ Life in the Trenches.

Speaking of life in the trenches, Pte. Povey said that now the troops took 48-hour turns in them. As regard's the food whilst in the trenches, the men had' not much to grumble about. They went in with a good supply of food, and were even enabled to have "hot" dinners. The dinners were made up ready, and all the men have to do was to warm them over candles, some two inches long. The same method was adopted for boiling water. Talking about' work with the. ,rifles, the Private said that for the first minute a man could fire 15 shots, but after that his weapon became so hot that the number of shots was reduced to 10 per minute. The maxim guns, however, could fire; 500 rounds per minute. At the end of the day's firing, the first job is to Ex bayonets ready for' any night attacks by the Germans. Prior to rejoining the colours Pte. Povey worked in the tanyard at Orford, from which

place upwards of a hundred men have joined the Army.

TIRING TRAVELS.

AVarringion Warrior Serves Long

Abroad and Returns Wounded.

After six months of active service, Private j. Bra'dbury, of the Lancashire Fusiliers, arrived home in Warrington about a month ago, suffer- ing from rheumatism and lung trouble. He is now recuperating at 51, Howard-street—where he lives with his wife and family. In peace times the private is employed in the cellars at Walker's Brewery. It was as far back as 1905 that Private Brad- bury first joined the Lancashire Fusiliers; and, out of the following seven years spent as a sol- dier, he served well over five in India.

* Afterwards he was placed on the Reserve, and thus was called up to join the regiment at Bury Barracks one day in August. They went up to Mons, but were, however, not in action there. Their first action was on the 26th of Aitust— when; in a fearfule hand-to-hand fight which lasted two hours, they lost a number of 31 ten. Private ,Bradbury had many narrow escapes. As time passed, the retreat was made gradu. ally from Mons to within sixteen kilometres of Paris. Then the tide turned. The advance began, and on the morning of September 13th another fight took place at St. Marguerite, the English losing almost half as many again as in the previous battle.. "Of course," explained the private, in an off-hand manner, "when we lose fifty • the Germans lose a hundred ! We are more accurate in our rifle fire." "The Only One to Escape."

Through a wood a little way in Belgium the '.private' along with others was carrying a wound,ed -enmrade on November 2nd. Suddenly the enemy covered the party with two shrap- nels. Three of the bearers were wounded, and the man they carried was blown clean out of the oil sheet. Private Bradbury alone escaped injury.

Fighting continued moderately in the trenches, and the private did his share gallantly. On December 7th, however, he found himself unable

to march properly; rheumatism had at last been brought on by his exposure to the rain and mud in the trenches.

He left, wet through, four days later. For sevehty-two hours he remained unconscious. "coming to" just outside Boulogne: he asked for a cigarette! For more than a week he re- mained in hospital at Boulogne, and was then removed to a convalescent home at Le Havre. Afterwards he was conveyed to another town. The private pays a high tribute to the French doctors and, nurses., who, he says, were most kind and sympathetic. On January 18th he sailed Or England', and the day following his arrival in this country travelled up to Scotland, where he remained in Aberdeen until leaving for Warrington. It is as well to add that, according to Pte. Bradbury, the English fighters and their Allies are, generally, very optimistic about the war, and expect it to finish in May. The Germans, however, fight only half-heartedly.

JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

A Striking Contrast. The gallant contributor of last week's par- ticularly interesting column on "Making good " soldiers" was frankly disappointed on finding that he had not been included in an early draft as he had supposed. His eagerness to be on active service reminded me' of a very different case that came under my observation during the Boer War. I was editor of an East Lancashire weekly at the time, and one morning I was way- laid by one of the linotype operators with the information that he had been ordered to hold himaAtin readiness to accompany a local Volun- teer contingent to South Africa. It was pain- fully evident that the official notification had reduced hilp to the condition forcibly, if not very elegantly, described as " blue' funk." He had long been a member of the Volunteers, but he assured me that he had not offered himself for the front—and, indeed, his protestations were quite superfluous. He was an excellent work- mat, and I really did not want to be deprived of his services even temporarily, and, convinced that there was some mistake, I reassured the fellow and sent him back to his task. During the day I inquired into the circumstances at the regimental headquarters, and the explana- tion was very simple. The corps had another of the same, name as• our " lino " man, and it was he who had volunteered for the war. By somebody's inadvertence, the notice had been sent to the former, andehad given him a shock from whjch he did not soon recover. Appar- ently ne was made of similar stuff to the duffers cynically referred to by one of the American humorists during the Civil War. Anticipating, a ballot for military service and fearing the re- sult. they had slipped over to the Canadian side. They were there taking their ease at an inn, when. one of the maids entered the room and closed the window. Tha weather was sultry, and one of the cowards demanded "why in thunder" she had closed 'the window on such a day. "Well,* said the girl. "I understood you gen- tlemen were afraid of the draft."

Quakerism and War. I have always genuinely admired the Society of Friends, and, especially after last week's accoulit of their noble work on war-stricken areas on the Continent. I would not be under- stood as criticising them at all adversely; but I can hardly help suggesting that their attitude in - relation to our armed resistance of the deliber- ate and calculated and unprovoked onslaught of the Prussian barbarian hordes is writ only ' illogical but puerile. Of course, we are all aware .that their principles are utterly at vari- ance with war as such, and we do not expect an Quaker personally to take up arms. They tell us that their viols are founded on the teaching of Christ as recorded in the Nely Testa- ment, and that war cannot be carried on with- out breaking many of the commandments—that armed resistance is wicked, and that the guid- ing pr,ineiple in life should be love. I make no attempt to gainsay this. As a matter of fact, it is the ideal of Christians of all denoraina- tiona; but unfortunately we cannot yet make the ideal into the actual. Besides, it is pOssible to err by too literal interpretation of the Master. 'lle Himself teaches that the law should, be kept in the spirit rather than in the letter; and assuredly He would have His professed followers do their utmost to vindicate honour, fidelity to covenants. justice and all else that He stands for, even if "he that bath no sword" has to "sell hl9, garment and buy one."

A Methodist View. A Wesleyan minister of my acquaintance re- cently stated the matter reasonably when a Doncaster Quaker resigned his public offices because be disapproved of his associates' favour- ing a. recruiting agency. Said my friend in • conclusion This Doncaster gentleman takes an impossible position when asked what lie would do if the Germans came and offered violent resistance in his housb. His reply is he would offer them a cup of tea and leave the issue to a Higher I'ower. This is absurd. Many Belgians offered "the cup of tea," but the Germans de- manded more, and then rewarded the hostesses who gave the cup of tea, with outrage and cold- blooded murder. While we sympathise with the Quakers in their difficulty, we have no sym- pathy with childishness, and a departure from the line of common sense. Our natural in- stincts are to defend our homes and our dear ones from the armed invader, and these in- stincts are given us by God, and He cannot intend u, to ignore them. Christ speaks of "the strong man armed who keepeth his palace," and the "strong man armed" is intended to teach aresistauee to the invader who ruthlessly breaks in to klll-and to destroy.

I mpracticable- It i 3 much too early in the day for the Quaker ' twilling to be practicable—though many of us cannot •hut agree with what those good and well- Jazeapix97, Salk have all along said as to.war •in the abstract. It should, however, be fairly ob- vious that it would have been the height of folly and pusillanimity—nay, worse—for us to , have stood tamely by and allowed the 'German bullies to ride roughshod over Europe. That would have meant the like-fate in reserve for us in our turn; and who will deny that we should have thoroughly deserved, such severe chastisement and complete humiliation? ,Instead of that, it is the Oerinans who are to be adequately pun- shed for their reckless and unforgettable wrong doing, and I, for one, rejoice in the thought that our own country is destined to play a- prominent and noble part in this supreme .and necessary and (as I hold) essentially Chris,, tian work. Is thp Kaiser Sane:- . This is another pertinent question, and it seems to me naturally to follow what I have just written. To say the least, the mountebank who has -too long been suffered to masquerade as the German Emperor is abnormal. To be sure,.as one pointed out some years ago, it is an exceedingly difficult matter to • draw the line which separates the sane from the insane. When we study the .diagnosrle traits df mental diselße.s. we ,begin to doubt whether some of those whose . mental processes we have, hitherto held in the highest respeet are not,-t6 put -it mildly, in questionable condition. If this is true of the average man how I:web more -of those whose acts make history? For ex- ample, Peter the Cireatand Napoleon the First were both epileptics, as was Julius Cwsar. Raphael was affected with suicid*l mania, Richelieu, on occasion, imagined himself a horse; Descartes was followed by a spectre; oCroinwell was a hypochondriac and had visions; J. J. Rousseau was a melancholy madman; 'S4w-edenborg (concerning whom Warringtonians have-of late heard particularly from an earnest

Continued in next column.

STUDY OF INTERNATIONAL RELATIONS

Sir,—Just before the outbreak of the war Viscount Bryce expressed the view that the provision of a wider and deeper education in international ,relationship had become an im- mediate and clamant necessity. Au educa- tional movement to meet this end is still more imperatively required to-day, when the great issues raised by the. war need an educated democracy for their right solution. Accord- ingly, with Lord Bryce's cordial approval and sympathy, a Council has been formed for this special purpose. Peculiar obligation rests on all men and women of broad-educational outlook to guide and inspire such an effort. Happily men and women everywhere are now showing a keen and widespread desire to study and under- stand the vitally important questions with which they are faced.. The opportunity thus presented is a great one, for the final sovereign power rests in the hands of the people, and to aid them rightly to use that power is true national service.

That Which gives most impetus to education- al work is the sense that it supplies the in- spiration and guidance for effective citizenship, and we therefore make a strong appeal for help in an educational campaign. The immediate aim should be to assist people to understand something of the issues taised by the war, the master ideas which lie at the foundations of our national life, the history of Europe; the philosophies behind policies, and the economic and ethical problems which the war will bequeath to the world. • Beyond this is the larger task of education in true feeling and insight, and the reconstrile- tion not merely of the material fabric of civil- isation, but also of its moral and spiritual basis.

Every church and religious institution, and every organisation for the betterment of life and the propagation of ideals, as well as every man and .woman of goodwill, may find scope for service in this great task. It cannot be done by any special group alone, but demands the combined wisdom and energy of all. Organisations such as the Workers' Educa- tional Association, the Nationaf Home Read- ing Union, the National Adult School Union, and others,. are already working along these lines. Whilst desiring to further the work of all such bodies, we seek to interest many be- yond their borders in this movement. Obviously such a task can be dpproached in many ways, each ,valuable in ,its own place. Experience has shown that one of the .most successful methods is that of study and dis- cussion groups. These are easy to form, suit- able for persons of widely different capacity and attainments, and singularly effective ,as a means of carrying out Mazzini's great concep- tion of "Education by Association." , Are there not many prevented by circum- stances from giving up their ordinary occupa- tions for service abroad; who will feel ita duty to make leisure for doing work of this pro- foundly important kind? ' Readers of this letter are cordially invited to help in one or more of the following ways: ta) By offering to lead such a Study Group. (It would help it any special qualifica- tions were mentioned in replying.) • (b) By offering to join and assist in forming a 'Study Group. (c) By offering the hospitality of their houses for such groups. d) By sending donations ,to help with the - provision of text-books and other ex-

pe- nses connected with the campaign

The- Council has accepted hospitality in the shape of temporary office accommodatiOn from the National Adult School Union: and has also been fortunate in securing the co-opera- tion of the British Institute of Social Service for purposes of reference and research. The following have consented to act as an Advisory Council : _ _

LYMM.

Broomedge United Service.—A united service in connection with the Free Church Council was held in the Broomedge Primitive ,Methodist Church on Wednesday evening. when th 9 Rev. E. E. Lovell preached An inspiring sermdn. . Changes at Red Cross Hospital.—Yesterday three more patients left "Brookfield." They were Corporal Ilickie (South Lancashires), Drummer Eade (North Staffordshires), and Private Roberts (Middlesex Regiment, the popular violinist). The trio are now on fur- lough at their respective homes. There is now room for two more wounded warriors at the hospital.—Sister Ilett, who has done such valu- able work as matron, and is held in great esteem, has this week resigned her duties. Miss Colburn succeeds to the position.

Primitive Methodists' Tea and Concert. —Over 120 gathered at the Primitive Methodist Sunday school on Wednesday evening, when a very suc- cessful tea meeting, promoted and carried out by the elder scholars, was held. The, young ladies provided an excellent repast. This hav- ing been partaken of, a grand concert followed. The Rev. R. W. Ferguson presided, and suitably addressed the audience during an .interval. A solo was given by Miss Millicent Clare, and the choir rendered several old English part-songs and a "Laughing Chorus" in fine style. Miss Lois E. Priestner recited. A good sketch, "The New Servant:" was given by Mrs. and Miss Lois Prietner; whilst those • two ladies, together with Miss Bessie Cross, Mr._G. W. .Cross and Mr. S. Simage earned loud applause with another sketch, "A Slight Mistake." By special request, two action songs were given by "The Red Battalion" (consisting of seven young ladies). The entertainment closed with the singing of "Auld Lang Syne." The Misses Shore and Mary Broady accompanied on the piano. The tefa and concert were promoted in order to reduce the chapel debt.• and the pro- ceeds amounted to 41:7 10s. 4d. Mrs. Priestner and Mr. John Edwards had chvge of the ar- rangements.

HOW LONG TO WAIT?

Lymm Soldiers In Reserve Trenches ,and Expectation. FOUR WOUNDED.

The Lymm men a& now in the reserve trenches, just behind the firing line, and—if one may judge from the many letters they have sent home—are "dying to get at the enemy." They give all sorts of interesting details re- garding their present situation. Private S. Gibbs, of the sth Cheshires, has written several letters to his wife at Henry-street, and one received on Monday said: "We are now only two miles from the firing line We go in the trenches for four days, and then have four days' rest in the village two miles be- hind." In another note received two days ago he writes: " We are getting a little more used to the Mace now, and can stand the reports of the guns. The Germans were shelling the town yesterday, and wounded four of our fel- lows—out of the sth Batthlion."

11(:1101111 A.ERIFIED.

I..min Soldiers In 'lrene lies—An Indescribable Tinie.

The following letter (just received by Mr. Frank Leach, of Hatton Cottage. Mayfield-road, Grappenhall) from Private Wallace McHugh, a Lymm Territorial now serving with the C Com- pany, sth Cheshire Vegiment, British Expedi- tionary Force, in France, verifies the rumour, mentioned in last week's "Examiner." respect- ing the Lymiu men being in the 'trenches. Terrible Travelling.

"We are soon amongst it. We have been travel- ling thirty hours in cattle trucks—forty men in each, packed. We were better off than some others—had a mouth-organ in ours, and stopped some time in the night for rum and cafee. We are now in a town that the Germans have been in, but driven out-a -gain. They have poisoned nearly all the water and plants, and made big holes in the road with 'Jack Johnsons! We are in a vineyard now—all hot-houses. "We are three. miles off the trenches. The guns are' going all the time, and aircraft is always in the air. I can't describe all the things I haye seen, the little time I have been het‘e. We have met -many friends, and dropped on the 'Terriers' straightaway. They are here with us. . . . "We Are Next On Turn." ,

"P.S.'(written later). We have moved again —farther into Belgium. .The guns are terrible, . There is a great scarcity bfematches and tobacco.. We are next on turn to relieve the - in the trenches. There' are very few people ropnd here: they have left. It's very hard to: keep cheerful at times. (Bang! The shells are at it again o'7 . . This letter, by the way. has been passed by rHptain G. W. C. llartley,. whose home. is at • Wormald Omen." Lymm.
