LA MAI " TERRIER " PROMOTED.

Sergeant Thomas Field Writes Us From Egypt.

VILLAGE'S FINE FIGHTING MATERIAL.

We have received an interesting letter from Sergt. Thomas W. Field, of D Company of the 7th (Territorial) Battalion of the Lancashire Fusiliers, who at the moment of writing was quartered at Kars-El-Nil, Cairo, Egypt„ and who, since writing, has no doubt played his part in smashing up misguided Turkey's army. Sergt. Field's home is at Newfield, Lymm, and he is well known and widely respected in the little Cheshire township, and his many friends will learn with pleasure that since going on active service he has been promoted. . The gal- lant Sergeant writes us as follows : Proud to Read,of Lymm's Share.

." From your issue of the Warrington Ex- aminer,' dated January . 23rd—which I Am pleased to inform you I had the pleasure to be reading here on February notice that Lymm 'Terriers' are busy preparing for the Front, with Sergt. Clare among them, and being a resident of Lymm, I was proud to read such good news, as I am sure they will render a good. account of themselves, for I know there are men among them who have had-quite a deal of ex- perience and training, which I,can assure you is absolutely essential. The real test of the value of a soldier is his degree of efficiency. . . . _ _

There are quite a dea of people who have a sort of hazy idea that when a man puts on the King's uniform he.,is fit • to fight three or four people of other. nationalities. Alas! that such things could be!

"It happens very frequently that a ' Terrier ' at home is challenged that he is useless unless he is at the fighting line, but before he can get there or anywhere else abroad, he must first sign a declaration agreeing to serve anywhere outside the United Kingdom, and when he has done this, he has .not the slightest idea when and where he may go, as there are more places than the frontier of France and Belgium where duties are hard and require the same degree of efficiency—as, for instance, .the place we. are in. It all depends on the strength of the 'Terriers'' Battalion and the kind of division that is being Made up, which place he will be allotted to. I had much the same kind of criticism thrown at me.

Would Like to Have Been with the Lymm Boys. I notice also that a good many Reservists and odd members of different units have left Lymm, much to their credit, and I think that the total must have surprised a good many people, who did not realise the amount of good fighting material that dwelt within hearing of the old .church bells. And I am pleased to learn that so 'many have come forward and hope to be able, 'when' I pick up - further issues of the 'Examiner' to read that even :more and more men are showing the same spirit. Personally, I should have liked to have been with the Lynim detachment, only my services were, sought on August 12th by the Battalion in which I am now serving, and which I left in 1912. I had the honour of serving with this Battalion in South Africa, when I was but 17, and . I cherish five clasps on one medal, to- gether with a good many friendships that were then fornied, and have lasted through the yhrs that have passed since we rounded up Kruger's crowd. I remember quite well when we Volun- teers were going out to South Africa that people said we should be useless, for we were only 'Saturday night soldiers.' But the Army has a knack of polishing raw material, a fact which has evinced itself in the stlite of the men who recently arrived in j.,ymm for a short holiday. Moreover, it has been proved time after time, in Vie present great Struggle, that the 'Terriers' are 50 per cent, better tl?an'the War Office gave credit for. Personally, I think the only fault with the ' Terriers ' is that there is not enough of them. And there could hive been if conce,•- sions had been granted' to the members in the past! It a man is in 'receipt of anything like a decent weekly salary, and he has to lose it whilst at camp, it makes him think twice before joining the 'Terriers,' especially if he has a wife and children, to keep whom demands his entire earnings. Ile cannot afford to lose, and the fact that so often he .would have to do so. is one of the chief reasons that has stopped many a good man joining. •

===== 0002601_19150306_art0135.txt | included=true | included =====
The Heroism of the Patient Women.

There are heroes elsewhere than in the ranks of those who go out to fight. What about the soldier's wife, for instance? From what my wife tells me of her .method of spending her Government allowance, so as to keep home to- gether while I am away, I can quite see that she could give me a decent lesson in domestic economy. It must come hard on many a sol- dier's wife to be alone, for her husband has some consolation in the. company of the chums serving with. him. Right enough, if she has children, they are a great comfort, but, on the other' hand, she has that fear which is the dread' of every true mother, -viz., that the children may ask-or require something that her allowance will not run to and which, were her husband by her side, she would only hare to mention and it would be there. "Three years ago I had a chance of a com- mission in His Majesty'S Territorial' Forces, which, Lam sorry to say, I-did not a6cept, but when the: beat of. the drum sounded the old spirit evinced itself: and, like many another man, I could not shirk- So I joined my old corps is a Private, and I was promoted to the rank of sergeant on Pctober 31st, 1914, at Cairo. "I know my wifeA.is.not the only ,one, which here compels me to .say 'Good luck' to the woman Who has the nerve to watch a man's home and his children while he is away doing a duty that so many -fireside critics are ever so grateful to be . absent from, and to these people if it was in my , power I-would -say '--Pight or pay,' and if they cannot pay, then fight." •

misigIONARY WORK .XMONGST

1,11)Elt S.

FA:111S THIS Of His Parish " In South Africa.

A numerous audience assembled in St. Thomas' Parish Room on Morahsy evening to listen to a lecture by the Rev. W. S. Evans, a missionary recently back from South Africa "and shortly to return to his sphere of. labour. "The ..I.,and of Good Hope" was the title of his lecture, which was illustrated with beautiful lantern slides,, many of them .reproduced from photographs taken by the Lecturer himself. The "Viciti (the Rev. J. Collins) occupied the chair, and at the outset a Yew items of business in connection with -the local auiiliary. of the Society for the Propagation, of the Gospel— under whose Auspices the lecture was given= were transacted, such business including the appointment of a representative for the Stock- ton Heath S.P.G. Auxiliary on the Ruri-decanal Committee, Miss Astall -being chosen to suc- ceed Mr. Rose, resigned.

The Chairman their referred to the subject of 'the lecture and remarked that it was un- necessary- for him to introduce the Rev.. Evans to a Stockton Heath audience, as the reverend gentleman hod been in the district for some little time and was already known to most of them. However, (hey would all she interested to learn. that Mr. Evans would be going back to .the mission field in a .few. weeks' time.

Going Back to the Mission Field.

In the course of his lecture the Rev. W. S. Evans said that it was quite true that he would be going back to the mission field, but he did not anticipate that -he would be going until about the end a June. Re felt that he wanted

to get into harness again; to still further realise his life's ambition, which had always been to do missionary work. (Hear, hear.) Whilst in Africa, he continued,- his work had been chiefly among the Kaffir lepers, who were segregated in isolation hospitals and compounds and who were never permitted to leave certain areas. In racy and interesting fashion the Lecturer then described a voyage out from Southampton to South Africa, illustrating the different phases of the long journey with a number of beautiful lantern slides, those de- picting life and scenes in Madeira being espe- eially lovely and interesting. A fine picture of the wonderful Table Bay prefaced the Lec- turer's description of life and work in South Africa. The South African veldt, he said, gave one an awesome idea of the immense in Nature, vast rolling plains stretching in every direction as far as the eye could see.

===== 0002601_19150306_art0138.txt | included=true | included =====
Some Wonderful Sights.

Some of the most wonderful sights in the world were to be seen in South Africa, and amongst the most wonderful of the may natural wonders were the glorious Victoria Falls. A photograph of the grave of Cecil Rhodes, "the man who made South Africa," was specially interesting, and became more so as the Lecturer briefly recounted the life-work of this great pioneer of empire, whose father was a clergyman. If, said the Lecturer, South Africa had its wonders, it also had its terrors, and. among 'these 'latter were .the plagues of locusts, which resulted in the wholesale destruc- tion of crops and the smaller vegetables. Cattle diseases—rinderpest and the like—and droughts were also dreadful scourges with which the South African agriculturist had to contend. Among the many interesting photographs shown one of the most interesting was that Of a witch doctor, and these gentry the Lecturer classified as among the worst terrors that South Africa holds.

===== 0002601_19150306_art0139.txt | included=true | included =====
Fewer Diamonds During War Time.

A lecture on South Africa would, of course, be incomplete without reference to the fabu- lously rich diamond mines of Kimberley, and the but little-less wealthy gold mines of Johannesburg, and naturally the Lecturer had much of interest to say about the gold and diamond mines, remarking that on account of the war the output both of precious stones and precious metals was considerably restricted. In conclusion the Lecturer said -that his parish was some GOO square miles in extent, and he had to cover it on horseback, riding from station to station, the result being that he thought himself fortunate to be able to spend even three or four days a month ,at home. Some idea of a day's round might be gathered from the fact that one Easter Sunday he set out on his pastoral Visitation at 2.30 in the morning and had not finished until eight o'clock at night. The usual rotes of thanks brought the pro- ceedings to a close:

WAR MOTOR TRIUMPH.

(Queries and correspondence should be ad- dressed to "The Hu*" c/o The Editor. Re- plies cannot be sent by post, and all queries will be answered in our columns.)

===== 0002601_19150306_art0146.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ALL-BLACK MACHINE.

The, extraordinarily wet weather of the last few months hits done more to convince me of the value of the all-black machine than all the catalogues ever issued by the :various manufac- turers. After a long and arduous run through slippery streets and muddy country ,roads, there is no more comfortable feeling than to know that when home is reached at last the cycle can take care of itself. Though perhaps not quite so pleasing to the eye as the " all-plated " machine of a few years ago, with its glittering metal work and positive desire to rust, the all- black represents one of the greatest advances ever made in the cycle industry. But even a perfect mount, if such may be said of any machine, may have its virtues grossly exagger- ated. For exaMple, some optimistic riders recommend the copious application of several buckets of water as the best cleansing agent. This is advice as bad as it is foolish. A wet cloth may do all the good in the world, but water in volumes is ,almost certain to work its way into the vital parts of the machine, for obvious reasons not -black, exposing bearings, &c., to the danger of rust, and hastening the day of difficult running and strange noises Trom the interior.

===== 0002601_19150306_art0147.txt | included=true | included =====
ACCELERATING FROM REST.

There is always a tendency when starting a motor cycle from rest to attempt to get into top gear as rapidly as possible. No rider who con- siders the life of his engine, however, would be guilty of such a practice., Many riders, as soon as the machine begins to move, slip in the second gear, and then after running a fvw yards they put the clutch out and get in to top. I believe the real reason for this practice is the dislike riders have for noise, and therefore they effect a change as speedily as they can. So that no unnecessary strain may be put on the machine, close your ears to the noise, and run thirty, forty, or as many yards as are necessary to attain a good speed and then, and only then, change the gear. The low gear is fitted for a definite purpose; by changing too quickly no use prpctically is made of this-; hence-the strain on the machine is very severe. A trial over a given distance, watching the speedometer the while, will soon convince riders that there is an exact time to change in order to obtain the hest results.

===== 0002601_19150306_art0148.txt | included=true | included =====
TIGHTENING LOOSE NUTS.

Nuts occasionally have. a habit of becoming loose, and although it is necessary to tighten them . at once, it is not always easy to find a means of so doing when on the road. There are ways however, in which they can be made to hold, but it depends to a great extent upon their size as to which method is adopted. A small nut may be hammered until it is slightly out of truth, when the • narrow part will grip the threads • of - the bolt. With larger nuts this metinid, is not practicable. As a temporary ex- pedient, a 'piece of string, varying in thiCkness according tp the degree of slackness and the size of the bolt. may be threaded through the nut, when the latter is screwed home.‘ This. will generally hold firm until home or a garage is reached. A better plan, imwever, is to use a narrow strip of emery cloth. This is used in

the same way as the string, but the roughness of its surface helps the threads to grip more tightly. These methods will answer for a time, but they must not be trusted for long:

===== 0002601_19150306_art0149.txt | included=true | included =====
BROKEN PETROL PIPES.

It is not altogether an uncommon thipg for petrol pipes to break, and sometimes a driver is at a loss to know how a repair may be.effected so that the nearest garage may be leached. On three occasions within the past month I have been stopped on account of this, and I have come to the conclusion that the conditions of the have something to •do with it. The thing, however, is to be able to mend the break, at any rate, for the time being. I have found the following method to answer the purpose: When. the break is in the pipe, away from the unions, a piece 'of rubber tubing is first of all slipped on to the two ends. Then wrap four or five strips torn from 4 handkerChief round the rubber; finally, lash the whole firmly together with string. The rubber tube may be dispensed with. I always carry a piece of soap with me in my tool kit, and if the linen is well/rubbed with this the join will be more lasting. Through such a bandage the spirit will leak very slowly.

THE SPARE WHEEL. In motoring, as in everything else, it is fre- quently the small things that run away with the money. We want, above all things, to be :- economical, but this does not imply meanness. True eConomy lies in the expenditure of a little. ' money, either to save more or to give greater efficiency in one. direction or another. flow , • many Cars that one sees on the road provide any :protection for the spare wheel? It is quite within the mark to say that only a very small proportion carry a cover for the spare; yet by the outlay of only a few shipings a good case can be secured. Tbe advantage of this addition is that it protects the tyre from rain and If the the tyre has been used and there are any cuts or gashes in it, the rain penetrating will cause it to deteriorate rapidly. In fine, weather. again, the tyre is damaged by the action 'Of the sun's rays in that they cause the rubber to perish.. A saving in the tyre bill at a small cost can be effected by 'haying a suitable wrapper for the spare wheel.

THE MOTOR CAR IN WAR. If the present European war has done noth- ing else it has certainly proved conclusively, the enormous value of the motor car. No sine!. factor has played so tremendously important a part. During a recent visit to the Front I was deeply impressed by the large number of car, employed, and by their extreme value to all branches of the service. The motor 'bua is there . in its hundreds; not only are these used for conveying stores and ammunition, but for the speedy removal of troops they are invaluable. Private ears for the carrying of despatches are greatly in evidence. Hospital cars are every- where to be seen. Even motor kitchens are used extensively. • The motor, cycle, too, has played its part splendidly. On the bad roads. often torn 'up in an almost incredible manner. they are frequently of More use than their four- wheeled rivals,. since they can wend their "way between ,the ruts and the holes. Perhaps the most surprising thing about it all is the truly wonderful wig in which the cars have stoopl as severe a test as it is possible to imagine. Very few complaints are to be heard of break-downs. save, of course, in the case of accidents caused by shells and rifle shots. The engines are never cleaned—there is no time for that—and yet they continue to do their work satisfactorily. Dur- ing the recent severe weather many of the motor 'buses had their Machinery running all night,, as had they ceased they would inevitably have been rendered useless by the frost. • Altogether the British and French motor car manufac- turers are to be congratulated upon the splendid manner in which their machines have stood the test and upon the important part that they have played in the war.

WARRINGTON'S LAST-MINUTE WIN.

Even Points in the RiAber with Leeds.

Notes by " Centre."

Warrington's return to form—about which the critics who, heretofore, had not, judging from their printed effipions, previously dis- covered that Warringto'n had any paPticular form to return to (albeit, we did not doubt it)' and, which : their, fountain-pens are now a-busied in lauding to the skies—would appear. to be no flash-in-the-pan sort of affair, . but something very real. Yes, it is real, and the more we see of it, the more real it becomes; indeed, 'So real is it, that on Saturday. the Wilderspool lads were in a measure table to wise out the humiliation of the biggest defeat' they have experienced this season, and, at the same time, make their way into the second round Of the Northern Union Cup Competi- tion. Such an achievement should spur them on, and, with a continuance of their recent form, we may hope, or, rather, .we -dare hope that—well, that they will go much further in the competition! Leeds were redoubtable opponents and they put up a big fight: But Warrington responded with a bigger and a better, and, at the end, the verdict was theirs, by the odd point in nine; though on the after- noon's play the margin should have been a larger one in their favour. But, what boots the margin—they won! Wherefore, let us, .who are enthusiastic patrons at Wilderspool, rejoice and be ex,ceeding glad.

NO " LUCK "- ABOUT WARRINGTON'S WIN.

'ray back last Christmas time Leeds fairly put it'across'". the Warrington boys, sending them home with ail adverse score of 35--nil to Ponder over. Well, they pondered Over it and evidently made up their minds that it slkould not occur again if they could help it, and at the very next opportunity they had of showing Leeds the stuff they were really made of, they 'answered them with point for Point, though, really, they ought to have won On that occasion, for they did the bulk of the chal- lenging, and certainly seemed to be in a win- ning position when the referee adjudged it necessary to stop the game owing to the water- logged condition of the ground. Still, _let the dead past bury its dead; Saturday's win was a triumph indeed, and, what's more, it was a deserved one. There was ,not a trace of luck about it—it was a thoroughly well-earned vic- tory, and it went to the better team, judging on the day's play. Indeed, Warrington played nich a game as• may well give the best teams in the competition pause, provided only they retain the spirit, the determination and the skill which animated and directed them ,in Saturday's encounter. Never have Warring ton been seen in better form this season, and never have they so thoroughly deserved victory. CHESTERS' BRILLIANT RE-APPEARANCE

The re-appearance of Chesters in the War- rington front line had not a little to do with the win. He played with a dash—a vigour and courage—that was positively infectious, and his coadjutors in the van could- not fail to respond where he so determinedly led. It was, indeed, a bit of luck for the home team that his superior officer had consented to his travel- ling up from the South specially to figure in this important match; for now, be it remem- bered, Chesters has temporarily abandoned the playing field in order to prepare himself for a more serious field. He is no longer Chesters, the Warrington forward, but is Gunner (les- ters, of His Majesty's Royal Artillery—a soldier preparing, hilf to take his place. in attacks ranks of those who are making thrilling-atl3 uleta , alternating with brilliant defences, on blood-stained field ' there are no r s of the Continent; where referees to call half-time, or to check breach; of rule. Chesters is no new hand at cup-ti e warfare and he. was the hero of the afternoo soli- tary try that the when he scored the one soli- match produced. It, is by

PROMISING START IN THE CUP CONTEST.

way of being a strange coincidence that on a recent occasion when another old Warrington player-turned-soldier—Sergeant Skelhorner—ap- peared in the ranks of the old club, he should have been responsible for some of the best and brightest play of the Afternoon. But.so it was.

LEEDS Ii.EAD AT HALF-TIME. Saturday's gariae, as wis only to be expected, seeing that it was a cup tie, produced a splen- did "gate," but it was noticed that many mem- bers went on to the popular side, presumably as a protest against the new policy of. charging them admissign to et-) ties during war time. Leeds were fortunate in the .coin-spinning pre- liminary, and elected to play with the sun be- hind them. Fearnley set the ball a-rolling, and Baker soon put Leeds on the defensive, but, nu= Tranter spoilt what appeared to be a good opening by kicking out. Warrington, however, were soon back. on: the attack again, but there was something lacking about their finish, and they were not able to press home their onslaughts. The first points fell to Leeds, Lewis scoring a penalty goal with a well-judged kick, following it up soon after- wards with a "ditto, ditto." Far from taking the heart out of the Warrington men, these re- buffs hut further heartened them, and they in- stituted a series of attacks which it took the Yorkshiremen all their time to hold. However, Warrington were unable to get even with their opponents, tried they never so gamely, and when the time for crossing over was signalled they were four points to the bad, the place reserved for their figures on the score-board being an Ominous blank. • HOME TEAM'S EFFECTIVE RALLY.

In the second half Warringto4 pulled them- selves together splendidly, and with the wind in their favour they were a constant thorn in the side of their opponents. Early on in the moiety Dainteth, with a beautiful kick, trans- ferred play to 'right under the Leeds posts, where a terrific .struggle ensued. It seemed as if War- rington must score, when Campbell took a hand in the business, and, with the aid of his fellow threequarters, got the ball away. Unproductive penalty kicks to each side followed, and play slackened down for a time, until a fine piece of tackling by Chesters en'abled the Warrington van to attack in. force. Steadily they forced their way up-field, rebuffs only spurring them to still greater effort, and at length their persist- ency was rewarded, Chesters getting over the line, amid deafening cheers, for the one and only try of the:afternoon. Here was a fine chance to level up matters, and have a point to spare, but Jolley failed to take it, his kick being a lament- able failure. Still :Warrington were not dis- heartened at their doughty full-back's failure to put on the minor points, and continued to peg away, with the result that in good time the winning points came, Jolley having the oppor- tunity in the last minute of redeeming his repu- tation as a sound punter, and making the most of that opportunity by scoring a splendid penalty goal. Nothing further was scored, with the result that when the final whistle sounded the score-board read-: Warrington, 1 goal 1 try (5 points); Leeds, 2 goals (4 points).

Ahhi

FINE WORK BY THE FORWARDS.

Once again were Warrington deeply indebted to their forwards, for it was their brilliance and persistency that, as much as anything else, won the game. They played fine football. Their dribbling was a delight to behold and puzzled the Leeds defenders not a little. les, there can be no gainsaying the fact that Leeds were in the main beaten by the libme forwards, plus their own inability to adapt themselves to what is called the cup-tie game. Cheaters' part in the work of the Warrington vanguard we have already referred to, and it only re- mains to say that he received loyal and valu- able support from each of the other five mem- bers of the line, the Cunliffes and Fearnley rendering specially valuable assistance. Young and •Dainteth played together in perfect har- mony and Brookes got in a deal of good work in the first half, Tranter ably, aiding him. Bradshaw and Baker more than once proved their value as reliable tacklers, and Jolley played one of the games of his career. Like the forwards, the Warrington backs, taken as a whole, Were also at the top of their form, their tenacious tackling and "spotting" tac- tics doing much to put the visitors off their game. Lewis played a. sound game at full- back for the Yorkshiremen, being fulj of re- source and not a little daring, making a num- ber of fine runs. With the exception of Hughie. Davies, the Leeds threequarters were quite a mediocre lot, and Sanders spoiled an otherwise good game by frequent infringements at the pack. , Godward was the pick of the visitors' forwards, and the faster the play, and the more 'deadly the tackling, the better he seemed to like it.

Taken altogether, it was a fine, exhilarating game, though it, is a pity that the referee should have been, caned upon to administer so many lectures as were found npeessary.

THE N.U. CUP.

Draw for the Second Itoond. WARRINGTON AGAIN LUCKY.

\ As in the Lancashire Cup, so in the Northern Union Cup, Warrington are having the luck of the " draw" up to the present! When the result of the draw for the second round was made known it was seen that Warrington were once more to appear. on their "native heath," their opponefits being Salford. Curiously enough, it was Salford whom Warrington were pitted against in the second round of the Lan- cashire Cup, and on that occasion our local lads were successful in piling up a score of 22 points to 10, but lost to Rochdale Hornets in the third, round. Will history repeat itself? Let us hope so—at least so far as the second round goes! This will be the fourth meeting between Warrington and Salford this season, Warrington having won the three previous games, the scores .being respectively: 5-0 (at Wilderspool), .22-10 (at Wilderspool, Lanca- shire Cup) and 11L--0 (at Weaste) It must be remembered, however, that in the game at-the Royal Borough the Salford med were Oven under suspension, and they only had a reserve side out; in fact, there was only ondifirst team man taking part. If Warrington can only reproduce that fine form which was shown against Leeds an Sat- urday, there should be no doubt as to their entering the third round. _ _ _ _

The r-esult of the draw : for the second roUnd was as followS:

WARRINGTON AWAY TO-DAY.

Will They Again Beat Swinton?

Wartington are away to-day, being engaged to Swinton, at Swinton. As selected, there are three alterations in the team, as compared .with that which did so splendidly against Leeds on Saturday. Gunner Chesters, of course, is not available, having rejoined his regiment, and Green is on the injured list. In the plaas of these two players Mather and Fairhurst will figure, whilst Taberner goes on the left wing in the threequarter line, vice Baker.

The teams-last met on December sth at Wil- derspool, when Warrington won by 5 points to nil. HANGED HIMSELF.

At the Coroner's Court on Saturday after- noon, Mr. Brighouse inquired into the cir- cumstances of the death of ,William Bruce Knight, 57 years of age, a general labourer, who resided at 64, Watkin-street, Warrington, and who was found dead at his home at seven o'clock the same morning. Nora Knight, the widow, said her husband had ij,nt been in good health for the past two years,ln September last his only sou joined the Army; and this circumstance appeared to worry him a great deal. During the past 31 weeks he had been unable to .follow his em- ployment consequent upon suffering from gastritis of the- stomach, .for which .he was being attended .by •, a dbctor: Deceased. had complained of pains in the head. On Friday evening he 'took some epsom salts and. retired to bed at 11 p.m, In the night he complained to her of severe pain in the stomach. About 6.50 on Saturday morning she missed him from bed and shouted downstairs to him. Receiving no reply, she went down and found deceased hanging by the neck by his belt, which was attached to on iron hook in the ceiling. A chair •; was knocked over; suggesting that de- ceased had stood on it and then kicked it from under him. Frederick Ward Corroborated.

The jury returned a verdict that deceased committed suicide whilst temporarily insane.

The Boy. Dad : Do you know what happens to little boys that tell lies? Tad: Yes. If they tell good ones, they get away with it.

DRAW AT STOCKTON HEATH.

Notes by " Linesman."

After several re-plays the clubs eventually got through the first round of the " Starkey " Cup- ties, and on Saturday all the clubs were fixed up in the League tourney. Unfortunately, or otherwise, the -Newton Congregationalists and. the 'Earlestown Y.M.C.A. appear to have devel- oped the habit of. excusing themselves as not being able to raise a team on the Saturday morning, and last week the Longford and Grappenhall Clubs were, disappointed in their fixtures.

„ The Paddington team caused the surprise of the daY, as they had the!audacity to visit the White Star team and defeat them on their own ground. No doubt this will cause much satisfaction, in the Fairfield United camp, as the White Star are their most dangerous rivals in the League chart; whilst the Mission and Longford Clubs'are in close attendance on each other; and are only waiting for a slip by either of the top teams.

At the monthly meeting of 'the Warrington and District League-on Thursday, at the Lion Hotel, Mr. Bate in the chair, a protest- was considered from the Orford White Star Club respecting their match with, Paddington. It was decided that the result should stand, and the matter of registration of players be left over until the next meeting, pending further inquiries by the Secretary.

The Newton Congregationalists, will not be able to play their. home fixture with Grappen- hall, as they are unable to get a team together: Out of thirty players registered with the League, eighteen have joined the colours, and several others save left the district and gone to some other employment. The coMinittee are holding a meeting on Monday night, when some efforts are to be made to get a- team to- gether /to enable the club to fulfil their League fixtures.

Owing to their cup-tie experiences the Cen- tral and Hollins Green teams 'ad4War- to like each other and stick close together, the Central having the pull by a ioal less scored against them.

Some of the mmaining clubs hiwe not lived up to their reptation .and for several 'reasons they have not been able to play ;their full strength, and thus •oeenpy lowly Positions.

- To-day the second .round of the Starkey " Cop Competition; willsbe.played.. It is expected that all the ties will produce keen and exciting, struggles. The most notable one is, that be- tween the White Star' and Fairfield United teams, on the ground of the former.

-The meeting of Paddington and the 'Earles- town Mission will, be ,keenly contested to the last kick of the match. -

The Hollins Green team showed consistent form in the three games they played .against the Central before they came., to a definite issne and unfortunately for them in their tie -,vitti Longford they will not be able to Conimand the services of Mellor, their centre-half. His absence, Will no doubt be -a weakening of the Greenites'. team. But the .Longford lads may do well to remember that the game is not won till the whistle blows!

The remaining tie is between Walion and Earlestown Y.M.C.A. teams, on the ground of the former club, and as the Walton team in- cludes 'several well-known local players they may be expected to extend their visitors to the full.

Fairfield United: Potter; Bebbington and Scott; Gleave, Hunter and Lewis; Long, Wood- house, Millward, Taylor and Cole. Referee, Mr. R. G. 'Brandreth.

The Congregationalists won the toss and played with a strong wind at their backs. Mill- ward set the ball rolling and the opening ex- changes were evenly contested. Following a visit•to each end without any success the visi- tors made a more determined- effort, and, after fifteen minutes' play', Cole centred finely from the line, and Woodhouse drew first blood. The home team were now seen to better advantage, but Bebbington, Scott and Potter were equal to al deMands, and at length the Congregational- ists- had to retreat to their own:quarters. After Holt had saved well, Mellor handled inside the penalty area. Millward, taking the kick, scored the second goal. Half-time score: Fairfield United, 2 goals; Elmwood, nil. The Second portion opened with an attack by the United, but the Brothers Phcenix and Holt showed splendid resistance, and after their lines had been cleared, the United again re- turned to the attack, and J. Phoenix was un- fortunate to-put the ball through his own goal. At this stage the United had much their own way-"and 'Woodhouse broke through and scored with a 'splendid drive. Following these reverses the Congregationalists roused up and made a bold bid for the visitors' goal, and on one occa- shin three of their forwards 'missed an open goal. Shortly after this Kendrick got through on his own, but his final shot was .a few inches wide of the post.....Millward scored a fifth goal for the United. Result: Fairfield United, 5 goals; Elmwood Congregationalists. nil.

WYCLIFFE AND THE WAR.

Large Audience Attend Choir Concert For Relief Fund.

Continual ' rounds of hearty applause were bestowed at the Wycliffe Memorial Hall on Monday evening. The occasion was a very fine concert given in aid of the local, war fund. With only one exception all the artistes were members of the Wycliffe Choir. Mr. J. W. Slack presided over a very large attendance.

===== 0002601_19150306_art0185.txt | included=true | included =====
A REMARKABLE FACT.

Speaking during an interval, the Chairman referred in terms of gratification to the size of the company present. It .brought them, he said, to the serious part of the Meeting—the object. On such an occasion it was somewhat difficult for them to realise that England was at war. It was a most remarkable •fact that, whilst the fighting continued abroad, those at home went about their daily work as usual. There could be no doubt that it was the ability of the Fleet that kept them in that happy con- dition, and they 'hoped that it would remain so until the Allies had gained the victory. The way in which men were killing each other was most horrible, , and those at home had one duty—the alleviation of distress and -kuffering caused by the war. They were greatly Indebted to the choir for so nobly coming forward to give their services- (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19150306_art0186.txt | included=true | included =====
A NOVEL PROGRAMME.

The programme was quite novel in hese days in that it contained no patriotic itcius—whio have formed the .principal feature 'of most similar entertainments. It opened with • the choir's imposing Part-song," The Watchword." Later, the dramatic scena, "Liberty" -(in -which the solo-part was taken by kiss M. H. Atkinson) and part-song "Comrade? Song of Hope" were given, • The latter "was splendidly rendered, the happy blending and effective balance of the voices being most noteworthy. The place of honour in the programme was occupied by a cycle or tuneful old,. English melodies, under the title of "Flora's Holiday." These were finely treated by Miss Elsie Hewitt, -Mrs. J. Smallshaw, Mr. W. Booth and Mr. Fred Fieldhouse. From start to finish, the rendering was delightful, the catch (" Pedlar") being cleverly contrived. • Mrs. H. Haviour, who had a cordial recep- tion, gave a masterly interpretation of Svendsen's "Romance" on the violin, and Mr. Edgar Balmford's 'cello solo,. "Kol Nidrei," was heartily applauded. The trio (violin, 'cello and piano) given by Mrs. Haviour, Mr. Balm- ford and Mr. Payton was, perhaps, one of the finest items of the whole evening. .

- Miss M. H. Atkinson was iii-splendid voice, and her treatment of "Joy ofiLife" proved an excellent encore to "Spring's Awakening." The beauty of Mrs. J. Smallshaw's rendition of "Summer Night" was greatly enhanced by Mr. Balmford's 'cello obligato ; and in "Valley of Laughter" Miss Elsie -Hewitt displayed the wonderful range of her vocal powers. Miss A. Hall was quite successful in "The Dear Home Songs." That rollicking quartet, "Four Jolly Sailor- men," was capitally interpreted by Messrs. F. Fieldhouse, H. Hawthorn, J. Ainsworth and A. Davies, and fully deserved the encore it received. Mr. H. Hawthorn was heard to advantage in "The Pretty Creature," and Mr. J: Poole gave "The English Rose" with much effect. Mr. A. Davies was 'recalled for "UP' from Somerset," and responded with "The Drum-Major," which also proved a '.very great t- succeSs.

Much credit is due to Mr. W. H. Payton, who had trained the choir and who conducted. The accompanist was Mr. T. P. Wallington.

"BETTER EVERY TIME."

On the suggestion of the Rev, T. T. James, Mr. Joseph Monks proposed a cordial vote of thanks to the choir, the Chairman, Mr. Payton and Mr. Balmford. He had been struck, he said, by the splendid talent which Wycliffe possessed,. and a bit of music was an excellent thing in these times. He was very grateful for the treat, and thought that the most delightful concert he had attended at Wycliffe. Councillor Hawthorn seconded, and said the choir were always willing tc? render assistance in any cause. It must be a source of gratifica- tion to Mr. Payton to know that he ,had uh a choir under his control. Throughout the year their services were highly appreciated. He thought it was Mark Twain who said there was only one good choir, and it was now defuaot. (Laughter.) But they had a good one at Wycliffe. Mr. Balmford was not directly con- nected with the choir, but he had given his assistance on several occasions.

The Rev. T. T. James then put the resolution to the meeting, saying that the choir succeed•-d in all it undertook. It was always making, progress, and everything it gave was better than the one before. -

• The Motion, was , carried with actlamation, and in replying' on behalf of all colverneo, Mr. Payton said the choir A-fere wuy pleased with the result of the conceit, and weuid• be greatly encouraged to continue their work. 3t was thought that the choir coac;e,rts might he revived—(hear, hear)—and pro 3. ,31 V they Vould be. ,He hoped they would not then be over- shadowed by the dreadful war. It -via:;' pleasing to know that as a, result of fbat effort there Would be a good balance to band,: over to the Meal War Fund. (Applause.)'

===== 0002601_19150306_art0187.txt | included=true | included =====
COOPERATIVE SOCIETY.

Nearly 1,500 New Members and a Big Increase in Sales.

The. 217th quarterly meeting of' the Warring- ton E.I. .Co-operative /Society, Ltd., was held in the Co-operative Hall on Monday. Mr. J. Upson presided, and, in, addition to the com- mittee, about a hundred members were present. The minutes of the preceding quarterly meet- ing were read and confirmed, and the President then moved the adoption Of the committee's report. ***ln doing so he pointed out that the. society had been instrumental in keeping down prices, and, owing to the keen buying of the manager, were even now able to retail bread to their • members at a lower price than that prevailing in other 'towns. , • Difficulties had been, and were'. still beolg, experienced with -.regard •to obtaining supplies, of coal, but in this direction it had been ,thought best to limit supplies to any one mem- ber at a time to two cwt., thus enabrrig even. the 'poorest of the members to hose &II equal ehance of getting a- share of the supplies which the society were able to obtain. The, report showed that the sales for the six months ended January 19th were .k 182,649 an increase of £19,018' over the correspondiig. period of last year. The sales for the twelve months were £352,926, an increase of £26,106 over 1913. The membership bad increased by 357 in the .three months, ,and by 1,476 for' the year, and now 'stood at 15,051., . The profits, after meeting all charges for in- terest and depreciation, and writing off exp.”, diture on fixed and rolling stock during the Six months, left a balance disposable, which again enabled the' committee to recommend a divi- dend' of 3s. 2d. in the S. on all purchases, in- cluding butchery, and to make the usual grant to the Employes' Chrift Fund, and fOr fc.tuca- tion. • _ _

The report and balance sheet were adopted: Messrs. Jones and Grearley were elected mem- bers'.delegates.to the C.W.S. quarterly meetings for the next twelve months, and Messrs. Eib- bert, Ilaigh and Randles were eleyted as .mem- hers'. representatives on the • Benevolent rund Committee. _ .

The recommendations of the toinmittee that approval be given to, the grant of 1.10 in goods made to the Warrington Infirmary for the special use of wounded soldiers, -stlo in goods to the Red-Cross Hospital at• Lymm, and £lO. to the St. John Ambulance Society towards the cost of a bed, to be called "The Warrington Bed" for wounded soldiers, were approved. In addition to this the committee have fur- nished throughout a honk' for the occupation of Belgian Refugees, in Bewsey-street, at a total eost of ,137.

EMPLOYES' WAGES DURING THE WAR.

A question was raised by one of ate members as to whether the committee had taken into consideration the employes' wages in view of the increased cost of, living, and the President stated that although this had not been done up to the moment, as no request had formally come to them, yet he promised that in the near future the whole question should be gone into.

The meeting closed at a quarter to nine. the business haring been completed in record time,

===== 0002601_19150306_art0188.txt | included=true | included =====
ANGLING.

Tarring ton Working Men's

Association. N.A. CENTRE.

THE GOWY.

There was au . improvement in the waters last week-end, and the Gowy was again.the favoured place. Messrs. Jolley and Bryers were the first to put roach over the scales this season. Mr. F. Jolley's roach was 'a beauty, . drawing 11. b. 7og. 7dr., and ought to stand well on the board. He prophesies that'he will beat it by ounces. If he does not know where to find them at the Cowy, the novice has very little chance indeed. His clubmates will all be pleased to ,hear of him putting something on the scales this Week- end, that will cause them all the trouble they wish to displace.: There is little doubt that, given anything like o reasonable weather this week-end, there will 'be more ,members at the Gowy than has been the case this year.

===== 0002601_19150306_art0189.txt | included=true | included =====
W,ALTON RESERVOIR.

Arrangements have been made again to fish this gplendid trout fishery. Members are par- ticularly requested to read the rules printed behind their day tickets. There are several alterations from last season. The tickets are free to all members of the Northern Anglers of Warrington, but there are not more than 20 to be issued in one day, and 60 in any week. If any member makes application for a day's fish- ing and all the tickets for the day are in use, he may have his date booked for a ticket by apply- ing to Mr. E. Brookes at headquarters, but no member is allowed to apply except on the date previous to that upon which he wishes to use the ticket. There are four watchers or bank- rangers selected, and members must produce their licences when asked for by the following: 'Messrs. J. Cox, A. Stout, J. Potter and A. Swarsbrick.

THE RULES FOR WALTON.

Licences are issued as under :

Persons residing in Warrington or surround- ing parishes, season. tickets 10s. 6d., day tickets Is. Gd. •

Persons residing outside the above radius, season tickets 215., day tickets 2s. 6d. Local angling associations are given -special privileges for their members. Licensee shall not use more than one ro4L Hours of fishing from 6 a.m.,to one hour after sunset.

No trout under 10in, in length shall be retained.

Not more than four brace of trout shall be taken and retaiired in one day. No trimmer, side line,"or night line shall be used.

Fishing with worm after April 30th is pro- hibited..

Wading is prohibited. The licensee must enter in a book kept for that purpose at the Corporation lodge near the Reservoir the date, description, number and size of fish caught, and must also state the bait used. (This information is essential for rd.-stocking purposes.) No ground bait shall be used. .

The licence must be produced to any Water- works official, or any of Sir Gilbert Greenall's gamekeepers on demand. On breach of these rules the licence shall become void and the fee forfeited. N.A. CONCERT.

It has been decided that the society are to arrange a concert for the local soldiers and sailors now serving, the whole of the proceeds to go to the fund for buying clothing, &c. The bills and tickets will be distributed as early as possible, and every member is expected to give every assistance to make it a success. Further particulars will appear in due course.

===== 0002601_19150306_art0190.txt | included=true | included =====

PENKETH.

" A Miterable Case."—At, the Warrington County Court on Thursday, before his Honour Judge Gurner, \ Alice Scrutton, wife of a sergeant-major .in the West Laneashires, brought an action against her neighbour, Samuel .Griee, .a. farmer, to recover -.4, _the value of three fowls, which, she alleged, had been detained five weeks. She also lodged an 'amended Claim :for 30s. for damages alleged to have been done by defendant's fowls to twelve- score of cabbage plants.—Mr. A. Browne am peered for plaintiff and Mr. P. S. Steel for the defendant, who counter-claimed for 2s. for corn alleged to have been consUmed by plaintiff's fowls.—Mr. Browne stated .that the birds were prize fowls, which plaintiff had had sent to her from Suffolk. While plaintiff ,was away at a Military funeral three weeks ago her. fowls got loose and were seized by defendant, who claimed 2s. for the damage they had done .to his corp.—After the evidence •of the plaintiff and :her seven witnesses, Mr. steel suggested that it was it ease which, ought never to have been brought 'to 'waste the time of the Court. He then proceeded to call five witnesses for -the defence,- and after they had, been heard his Honour said that it was a miserable case of bitterness between bi-° women and he agreed with the suggestion Of Mr. Steel.—An order Was made for the defendant to return the fowls and for each side to pay their own costs. • • / •

AN OVER PRODUCTION OF POISON.

Some people produce uric-acid twice as fast as others, and an overload of this boisp.n is a serious thing for anylme. It comes in Itifferent ways, but the most productive causes are over- exertion and eatiiig too lunch, particularly of, meat.

Some allowance • should be made, to those suffering from uric-acid complaints, for they can't' help being nervous, morose, cross, 'f-uspi-' eious, headachy, dizzy; at times, and racked' ,with all sorts of queer pains. The rhe.umatic sufferer knows what real pain is like, b.nd it is no wonder he grows old too fast—and sooner or later develops heart trouble, sciatica, gravel, dropsy, or hardening of the arteries.

The sensible thing to do is to take warning at the first sign that uric-acid is developing—eat less meat, and not too much of any food.. Drink 'plenty of both milk and ,water. Take regular exercise, and get eight hours sleep seven nights a week. • •

30 SHOPS & STILL GR

WAR ITEMS. 1

FREE LIMBS FOR SOLVERS. The War Office announce that disabled sol- diers who are certified as requiring artificial &c., and surgical appliances should write to the Commissioners of the Royal Hos- pital, Chelsea, who are authorised •to supply them at the expense of the public funds. - HINT TO TRAM TRAVELLERS.

Get into the habit of placiug a small gum every day into the boxes on the. cars." This excellent advice has just appeared on the re- verse side of many local tram tickets. It refers,' of course, to the. ticket boxes—which have for some time been used as collecting boxes.for the National Relief Fund.

, Other. tickets give this advice: "If you can- not fight, you can pay!' Remember the boxes on the Car." OUR SEMI-DARK • STREETS.

Following in the wake of other large towns, Warrington, on Thursday, in compliance with the commands of. the military authorities, be- gan a course of reduced public and private lighting. The main thoroughfares, due in part to the fact of its being early closing day, wore a somewhat weird and dismal appearance. There was a- large proportion of unlit public lamps, but with 'ordinary care one had no difficulty in reaching home safely. No doubt, after a few, nights' experience, one will be- come used to the semi-darkness, and realising that it is in the interests of the country that the regulation is imposed, will not be disposed to grumble. A Stout Heart.

At the ,usnal Sunday afternoon assembly ,of the Wye/4(e P.S.A. Brotherhood, the Secretary (Mr. B. Farrar) read a letter from one of the members who is now serving his country with the 3rd Devons—Private T. -Yeates. ,

, Private Yeates, it will be recalled,, has al- ready seen active service at the Front and came, over to England some weeks-ago suffer- ing from wounds: He stated, in the course of bisletter to Mr. Farrar: "1 am leaving . . .

for the Front. . . . I am always ready to do my duty when called upon . . . and I. am sure that I shall go out this time with the same spirit as I went the first time, tnisting that God Will 'once again bring me back alive. We must trust in Him and look at the bright side of thing's in times such as these are. . I hope the P.S.A. Brotherhood is still going strong, although I suppose most of the young men have enlisted. To fill up these places you must bring in the older ones and keep the thing going until we come hack from the Front. . . . . What a glorious Sunday it will .be when we are all together. again— soldiers of the King,. and, even more and bet- 'ter, The gallant soldier added.e few words thank- ing kind friends who had sent him a ,present of. ,woollen ." comforts," end signed. himself "Brother Yeates." .

SHORTAGE OF DYES.

The Scheme Now Before the. Country. A.t a .meeting of the Central Executive Com- mittee of the .Employers' Parliamentary Asse- ciation, held in Manchester,' Mr. W. Peter Ifylands presiding, it was resolved :—" That in the opinion of this meeting the scheme now before the country to deal with the Shortage of dye-stuffs .ought -to be whole-heartedly sup- 'ported by all directly or indirectly ,concerned in the questi* and that this committee is strongly of opinion that it ig the duty of the Government to delegate forthwith to. the Royal Society (which it is believed • is willing - and anxious, to assist) the duty of organising the chemical talent now available in the Univer- sities, Technical Schools and University Col- leges in the couptry, with a view to their ser- vices being utilised in the research work that will 'he necessary for the successful' carrying on of the sehtme."

PR4)11) OF OUR " BOYS."

But %re The People At Home Doing

Their DutN ?

Writing in the current issue of the St. James' (Latchford) Parish Magazine, the Vicar (the Rey. 11. B. Firth)- says:—

As the months roll oil the war continues to be waged with unceasing -violence, and it is idle for anyone to predict the date of the,Peace which all the world is longing for: Meanwhile our interest in the course of the war becomes more intense and more personal, for very many of the young men of our parish are now on French soil and ar% either fighting or pre- paring to fight for their King wird country. We . shall do well to remember that they are fight- ing for us, too. Our enemies do not scruple to assert that England is the supreme object of their h'otred ; and were it not for the narrow strip of ocean that meparates England from Belgium we should be exposed to the same, deadly blows that have fallen', so heavily and so. tragically upon ,the Belgian people. We cannot but feel anxious about our brave 'fel- lows who have gone so cheerfully to defend the liberties of their. Country and to take their part. in this terrific conflict, but we do feel intensely proud of them and we should bear their names continually before the Throne of God. Are 'we doing our duty in this respect? Are the wives and the mothers of, our soldiers and sailors crowding to our churches on Sun- days, when intercessions are .always offered on behalf of our men? IS there a large number of communicants at our Wednesday morning celebrations? Is the solemnity and danger of the present critical situation realised? Is there a marked increase in sober, steady ways of living? These are grave questions which can, not all be answered in the affirmative, and Lent should make us pause and face the facts' as they really. are. That we as a people ought to be much more serious I do not doubt; that we as- a nation ought to be infinitely more prayerful 'I am painfully *conscious. Let, then, this Lent do its proper work for us itind increase., within us the spirit of penitence and ,the, practice of prayer.-

REV. A. F. MATTHEWS

Asks " What Is The Matter With The

World ?"

The Rev. Fra»cis John Matthews, of Glossop- road Baptist Church, Sheffield, visited the scenes of his former successful labours on Sunday and preached to large congregations attending- the choir anniversary at Latchford Baptist .(jhurch. In the evening the reverend gentleman preached on the question "What is the matter with the world?"

People., he said, seem to be afraid of facing the fact that "ideas" tell, and nothing in this world is more forceful than the nature and influence of religious notions. When, there- fore, One has to diagnose .the conditions of modern Life, national and European. in order to discover what is the matter with the world, 4 is seen that there is _a war of ideals_ and ideas. On. the one side' there is the spirit of arrogance, intolerance and overwhelming con- ceit in consequence of material advance; and oii.. the other' a passion for what- makes char- acter the most. noble and enduring factor in the world's best life. Both Germany and A.therica were Suffering from swelled-head, and the selfishness of the latter was only equalled by her Government's callousness to the extra- ordinary sufferings of the Belgians and their Country. America had few convictions beyond those associated with wealth and the .power it brought; and it .was' to her eternal shame- and disgrace that she had uttered no official protest against the outrageous and devilish conduct of Prussian militarism. A large section of man- kind had obviously lost its grip of God and human goodness. Only .a return to these truths in their beauty and fulness was able to bring back sanity and common-sense into . our national" and international relationships. The silly and wicked doctrine of the "Balance of Power" was the attempt to play off one nation against another. No great country in Europe was exenapt from the responsibility of advocat- ing and practising it. Murderous and Hateful War.

Jealousy, international misunderstanding, feuds, subterfuges and diplomatic lying of every form and description ran rife under its shadow, and now we were all caught in one of the most twirderous and hateful Wars that mankind had ever known. Surely there was need for Christ to come here' and now, not in, the sense that many would have us believe, and in which they would find their highest joy and comfort; but in the • rehabilitation of the .world's life in righteousness and true holiness of -character. No doubt after this .terrible crisis was over we should have to keep swatch and ward.' that the perfect peace of God Should come, and with it the goodwill of all men to- wards each other. The times were more than ripe for an advance in patience, goodwill, sound judgment, intellectual and spiritual • freedom for the individual and the race. The ap- proaehing.end' of the ago was the re-birth of a new world, in which .righteousness shall for ever dwell.

lie thought that the close contact into which the war would bring men of many nationalities would lead to a better understanding -between the peoples of the nations, and so help forward the, coming of the good time for which all good •men *OM

Beerhouse, iii Dallam-lane, Warrington, where .she espied a jacket hanging up in the kitchen. ,It..was from the pocket of this jacket that she took the watch and subsequently went with it to a pawn- broker's, whose manager. I HEREBY ORDER that the following reduc- tilet shall be made on the LIGHTING OF THE COITNTY OF CHESHIRE, INCLUDING THE BOROUGHS, except that portion com- prising the Mersey Coast Defence In all the brightly lighted streets, specially in the centre of ..he Towns and Boroughs, the lights must either be lowered or made invisible from above by shading them or by painting over the tops of and upper portions of the globes. WOOD, I now wish to thank you with all my heart for first giving me an excellent training in Typewriting and Phogography and then finding for me a position which, compared with the one I had when I began to attend your College, is much more congenial. affords infinitely better prospects, and has an immensely higher salary attached. Again thanking you, and hoping that I may have lots of opportunities of recommending you'r splendid College, I beg to remain, yours sincerely, IV. C. SUCH. Wood's college BOLD-STREET, WARRINGTON.
